Cortigiana onesta (Italian: "honored courtesan") was a term used for a specific category of prostitutes in Renaissance Italy. They are primarily associated with Rome and the Republic of Venice, though they were also present elsewhere in Italy. As a category of sex workers, they were distinguished by their self-presentation, which featured lavish consumption, luxurious attire, and an education aligned with the Renaissance ideals of the time. They have been compared to the hetairai of antiquity. Many courtesans of this type served as models for renowned artists and as muses for famous writers and poets; some were poets themselves.


Description


Identity and Role
A cortigiana onesta was a courtesan. She distinguished herself from other sex workers by having received a refined education and by having learned to dress, behave, and speak like an upper-class lady; a deliberate strategy to set herself apart from other prostitutes.
A courtesan of this type catered to clients from the upper classes — often influential men within the Church or the state — and frequently managed to circumvent the laws and regulations imposed upon other prostitutes.
The cortigiana onesta was known for embodying the contemporary ideal of the Renaissance woman—a *puella docta* (learned woman). She was educated in literature and the arts, and has been compared to the hetairai of antiquity. Many of these women became famous as public figures in their own time; among the most renowned were Tullia d'Aragona and Veronica Franco.


Work conditions
Courtesans belonged to the small minority of sex workers who could become wealthy through their trade, whch was a rarity in the profession. However, because a courtesan was expected to maintain a luxurious lifestyle as part of her professional role, saving money proved difficult. Consequently, many courtesans were unable to retain their earnings; by the time they were forced to retire, they had no savings left, and thus frequently ended their lives in poverty.  
A courtesan who managed to save money and retred with a fortune was described as a "prudent courtesan", which was not unknown, but described as rare.  
Courtesans also faced specific risks, often at the hands of jealous male clients. Two such risks were particularly notorious:

sfregia — the act of ruining a courtesan by slashing her face with a knife;
trentuno or trentuno reale — gang rape, which allegedly involved 31 or 79 men, respectively. This left the courtesan unable to continue commanding high prices and transformed her from a courtesan to an ordinary prostitute.


History
Rome and Venice were well-known centers for their culture of courtesans, though such women were also active in other parts of Italy. Tullia d'Aragona, for instance, was active in Florence.


Rome and Venice
The term is known from the second half of the 15th century. 
In Rome, the period spanning the reigns of Popes Alexander VI and Leo X is considered the era of the courtesans. 
The profession emerged in Rome toward the end of the 15th century, as the papal court grew increasingly magnificent and placed greater emphasis on formal display. 
This coincided with a broader trend across Renaissance Europe, where princely courts developed more elaborate public-facing representation; a form of representation in which the presence of women was considered essential. 
This posed a challenge at the papal court, however, where the men were predominantly clergy and thus had no wives to bring to receptions, and the Roman noblewoman traditionally lived in a high degree of sex segregation. 
Consequently, they began bringing along hired women to fill this role — women who were expected to conduct themselves with elegance. Imperia La Divina has been described as the first of these.
Courtesans also played a particularly significant role in the Republic of Venice. Venetian culture dictated a very secluded life for the wives of the men who governed the Republic. In Venice, prostitution had been confined, between 1360 and 1422, to the Castelletto district, and later to Carampane in the Rialto area. During the 16th-century Renaissance, a distinct culture of courtesans emerged; these women were educated according to Renaissance ideals — known as puella doctae — and were referred to as cortigiana onesta. 
Among the most famous of these are Marietta Mirtilla, Giulia Lombardo, Cornelia Griffa, La Zafetta, and Veronica Franco.


Decline
This class of courtesans was strongly associated with the Renaissance. As ideals shifted, the public role that these courtesans had occupied during the 16th century vanished; by the 17th century, prostitutes no longer held such a visible position. 
In Rome, courtesans were first ordered to be expelled in 1523. Although this ban was not strictly enforced, the public culture of the courtesan was definitively eradicated after the popes of the Counter-Reformation came to power following 1555.
In 1566, a papal decree ordered all prostitutes to leave Rome within six days; a directive that was, on this occasion, actually carried out. Twenty-four of the most prominent courtesans departed the city immediately; however, many of the women who followed — the means to pay for an armed escort — were robbed and murdered by highwaymen just outside the city.
In Venice, by contrast, the phenomenon persisted throughout the remainder of the century.


References

Trolls are a fictional species of tusked humanoids in Blizzard Entertainment's Warcraft franchise. First introduced as in-game units in the real-time strategy game Warcraft II: Tides of Darkness, trolls in Warcraft include several humanoid peoples with distinct cultures and political allegiances. The Darkspear trolls ally with the Horde in Warcraft III: Reign of Chaos and became one of the original playable races of World of Warcraft. Other troll peoples appear as opponents, while the Zandalari became playable as of Battle for Azeroth. Trolls also feature in Hearthstone, including its expansion Rastakhan's Rumble.
Blizzard developed the Darkspear as a jungle troll tribe attempting to change its ways in Warcraft III, distinguishing them from other jungle trolls when making them playable in World of Warcraft. Later development included updated models for Darkspear characters as well as the introduction of the Zandalari cultural origin, clothing and equipment drawing on Pre-Columbian era Mesoamerican cultures. For Midnight, the developers sought to expand the Amani beyond their earlier appearances as enemies and depict members of the group as heroes.
Scholars and critics have identified Black Caribbean, particularly Jamican, coding in the early Darkspear portrayal, including their speech, appearance, music and references to Vodou. Their analyses discuss colonial stereotypes, the association of Black identity with savagery, and the treatment of religious traditions. Commentary on female troll designs has examined sexual dimorphism and representations of Black Caribbean women, while ethnographic research has considered female troll avatars in relation to queer identification and desire. Research on the Brazilian Portuguese localization has also examined how players associate troll speech with regional identities and social stereotypes.


Concept and development
Trolls first appeared in Warcraft II: Tides of Darkness. Artist Samwise Didier recalled that the sequel broadened the armies beyond the orcs and humans of the first game. Rather than making every Horde soldier an orc, the team assigned different roles to other fantasy peoples, including trolls.
In a 2003 interview for Blizzard Insider, creative director Chris Metzen attributed the decision to make trolls playable in World of Warcraft to the developers' affection for them and their existing role in the Horde's story. He distinguished the Darkspear, a jungle-troll tribe introduced in the Warcraft III demo, from other troll groups. Metzen described the jungle trolls as evil, but said the Darkspear were trying to change. Metzen said that trolls would share the orcs' starting area, with troll inhabitants and a nearby village intended to give players a stronger presence within the orc homeland.
In its account of the Italian localization, Blizzard said that the team used Neapolitan as the basis for troll speech. The company attributed the choice to perceived parallels between troll culture and Neapolitan traditions concerning ancestry, superstition and streetwise behavior.
During the 2014 update of the male and female troll models, senior art director Chris Robinson described the difficulty of adapting facial models and animations to the different tusk options.
At BlizzCon 2017, senior character artist Christopher Chang described a new approach to the Zandalari for Battle for Azeroth. Their environment, clothing and equipment drew heavily on Aztec and Maya cultures, with gold and elaborate carvings used on armor and weapons. Prop artist Ashleigh Warner described revisiting earlier Zandalari designs and combining these influences with dinosaur imagery.
For Midnight, game designer Jake Shillan described expanding the Amani beyond their earlier portrayal as enemies in Zul'Aman. Lead quest designer Rachel Bott said that the expansion provided an opportunity to show the Amani as heroes, with leaders who disagreed over how to protect their people.


Depiction
Trolls are depicted as humanoids with tusks and two-toed feet; early playable models have blue or green skin. The franchise distinguishes several groups, including forest, jungle, desert and ice trolls. In Warcraft II, troll axethrowers serve as ranged troops in the orcish Horde.
The Darkspear are jungle trolls who ally with the orc leader Thrall during Warcraft III and follow the Horde to Kalimdor.  Darkspear trolls are one of the original game's eight playable races, and belong to the Horde alongside orcs, tauren and the undead. Their background in World of Warcraft describes a people displaced by other troll tribes, who establish a settlement on the Echo Isles off the coast of Durotar.
The Amani forest trolls led by Zul'jin, who were once aligned with the Horde during the events of Warcraft II, oppose the elven civilizations centered in Quel'thalas. Their stronghold, Zul'Aman, a combat encounter for groups of players or "raid", in World of Warcraft: The Burning Crusade.
The Zandalari and their homeland, Zandalar, were developed further for Battle for Azeroth. Zandalari trolls became playable in the expansion's March 2019 content update. Their culture includes the worship of loa, spirits venerated by trolls, and the use of dinosaurs as mounts.
World of Warcraft: Chronicle describes dark trolls being transformed into night elves by the magical Well of Eternity, a connection earlier material presented as a disputed theory. A 2026 Midnight storyline introduced a claim by the haranir people that trolls and elves share common ancestry. Quest designer Keith Riley clarified that this account represented the haranir's understanding of their history, rather than Blizzard's definitive genealogy.
Trolls are also the focus of the 2018 Hearthstone expansion Rastakhan's Rumble. It depicts a tournament in which troll champions lead teams under the patronage of loa.


Reception and analysis


Racial and religious representation
Jessica Langer's analysis of early World of Warcraft identifies Black Caribbean, particularly Jamaican, cultural coding in troll speech and appearance. She also associates Sen'jin Village's music, thatched huts and ritual masks with Caribbean imagery, and discusses references to Vodou in dialogue and quests. She places these depictions within a history of Western representations that ridicule the religion. G. Willow Wilson argues that the game's sympathetic treatment of formerly monstrous peoples can reproduce the stereotype of the noble savage. She cites the trolls' Afro-Caribbean accents, cannibalism and voodoo jokes, and skull-decorated villages. 
In Wired, Clive Thompson discussed the trolls' Jamaican accents and reported an analysis of the game's prewritten emotes by Beth Cox, supervised by Aaron Delwiche. According to Delwiche, Cox found that trolls were more likely than other races to make violent or sexual statements. Steven Tuttle and J. Talmadge Wright examine the language of the trolls' official racial profile, their Jamaican accents and their association with savagery. They question why characters they read as Black are singled out as savage in a game where players routinely fight demons and dragons. They connect this characterization to American media stereotypes of Black people, particularly men, as violent or criminal, and draw on Langer's discussion of the disparagement of Vodou.
Christopher Douglas uses troll religion in his criticism of the game's treatment of race and culture. He argues that World of Warcraft treats cultural identity as an inherited racial property rather than something learned. Douglas contrasts its use of popular stereotypes of Vodou with the specific references to Ernest Hemingway in a hunting quest sequence.
Writing in PC Gamer, Leana Hafer criticizes the Darkspear's exaggerated Jamaican speech and discusses the Zandalari's borrowing from Indigenous American cultures. She objects to their gods sharing the name of the spirits of Vodou, comparing this with the hypothetical adaptation of Jesus Christ into the humans' religion. In her view, these portrayals extend less respect to non-Abrahamic traditions and can imply that the cultures being represented are less human.


Gender and sexuality
In an analysis of sexual dimorphism reported by Annalee Newitz in Wired, Andrea Rubenstein contrasts male trolls' large tusks and posture with females' much smaller tusks and different posture. Newitz criticizes the limited options for female bodies and the application of a slender feminine ideal to fantasy creatures. Langer examines jokes about female trolls' appearance and male trolls' sexual preferences. She argues that the women are presented as both unattractive and hypersexualized, with dances resembling Jamaican dancehall. She relates this combination to colonial representations of Black Caribbean women.
In her ethnographic study of queer play, Jenny Sundén describes female trolls as performing a rough-edged, relaxed femininity with rebellious undertones. She discusses their husky laughter, dancing and boastful flirtation, and her own attachment to a female troll character. Although she does not regard female trolls as conventional beauties, Sundén finds her own character attractive and describes her proud bearing. She considers attachment to one's own and other players' characters an important part of play, involving both identification and desire.


Language and localization
Heber de Oliveira Costa e Silva examines players' responses to troll speech in the Brazilian Portuguese localization. In the forum comments he analyzes, players associate the trolls with regional and social identities, including those of Bahia and Rio de Janeiro. Silva identifies references to capoeira and Olodum among the Bahian associations, and prejudiced assumptions about slang, weapons and allegedly incorrect speech among the responses to a perceived Rio accent. He observes that comments associate trolls with cunning and deceit, although the game assigns that role to goblins. His analysis relates praise and criticism of the localization to players' expectations about the identities and behavior of the fictional races.

Bibliography
Blizzard Entertainment (2005). World of Warcraft: Game Manual (PDF) (European English-language ed.). Blizzard Entertainment.
Langer, Jessica (2008). "The Familiar and the Foreign: Playing (Post)Colonialism in World of Warcraft". In Corneliussen, Hilde G.; Rettberg, Jill Walker (eds.). Digital Culture, Play, and Identity: A World of Warcraft Reader. Cambridge, Massachusetts: MIT Press. pp. 87–108. ISBN 978-0-262-03370-1.

Kim Dai-Sung (Korean: 김대성; Hanja: 金大成; 1930–2007) was a South Korean glass engineer and industrial adviser. After nearly two decades in the Korean glass industry, he built a glass factory at Shah Alam for a Malaysian police cooperative in 1975, which the Kyunghyang Shinmun described as Malaysia's first. He spent 24 years in Malaysia, where he later advised Korean companies and the Malaysian government agency Majlis Amanah Rakyat (MARA). In Malaysia he was known as "Uncle Kim" and as "Harimau" (Malay for "tiger"), rendered "Harimao" in a 1995 YTN documentary about him. In retirement in New Jersey he ran a volunteer program for Korean immigrants.


Early life and education
Kim graduated from the College of Commerce of Seoul National University. During the Korean War he worked with the United States Marine Corps, where he learned English.


Career in Korea
In 1955 Kim and his uncle, the industrialist and National Assembly member Kim Bong-jae, founded Dae Han Glass (대한유리), with Kim Bong-jae as its representative director. Kim worked there for about twenty years, first as plant manager and then as managing director. On 15 August 1963, as managing director of Dae Han Glass, he received the Silver Tower of the Order of Industrial Service Merit.


Malaysia
In 1972, while returning from business in the Middle East, Kim stopped in Malaysia and met the Deputy Home Minister, Samad, who asked him to build a glass factory for the country. He became technical adviser to the welfare cooperative of the Royal Malaysia Police and in 1973 signed a five-year contract to build and run the plant. The factory, operated by the cooperative's subsidiary S.K.P. Gelas Sdn Bhd, was built at Shah Alam with Korean-made machinery and technicians brought from Dae Han Glass, and was completed on 31 December 1975, six months after construction began, at a cost of M$3.3 million. By 1977 it produced about 5,000 glass items a day, had reached M$1 million in sales in its first eleven months, and had trained 35 Malaysian technicians. S.K.P. Gelas appears among the Korean investments in Malaysia recorded up to 1980. Kim left the company on 1 July 1980.
In the years that followed he headed the Malaysian subsidiary of POSCO, advised Samsung Engineering, and held a director-level post at a Malaysian industrial development agency; he stayed in Malaysia until 1996. A 1995 YTN report described him as chief adviser to MARA and its only foreign staff member.


Later life
Kim moved to the United States in 1996 and settled in Edgewater, New Jersey. In September 2002 he and his wife joined the AmeriCorps program of the International Institute of New Jersey as its coordinators for the Asian community. Working with the Fort Lee Korean American Association and the Fort Lee Police Department, he taught free English classes for adults, Chinese classes, and drunk-driving and youth crime prevention courses. The association gave him a plaque of thanks in December 2002, and the Fort Lee Police Department and others honored him and his wife at the program's graduation in August 2003. He spoke Korean, English, Japanese, Chinese and Malay.


Personal life
Kim married Son Kyung-hee; they had one son and two daughters.


References

The 1948–49 Scranton Miners season was the third season of the franchise and the second season (and first full season) of the Scranton Miners specifically from Scranton, Pennsylvania in the revived (yet weakened up) form of the American Basketball League. Entering this season, Scranton would be one of eight teams competing in the league once again, though not the exact same eight teams from the previous season due to the Bridgeport Newfield Steelers replacing the EPBL's Lancaster (Red) Roses and the Philadelphia SPHAs initially moving to Atlantic City, New Jersey to become the Atlantic City Tides at first before returning to Philadelphia, Pennsylvania to become the SPHAs once again for the rest of the season not long afterward. Originally, Buck Freeman from the University of Scranton would try and coach the team while also coaching his college basketball squad, but after only two games coaching the Miners, Freeman would resign from his position in the ABL, which led to him being replaced by two different player-coaches in Red Wallace and Bernie Sarachak for the rest of the season. Despite the sudden changes throughout the season, Scranton would end up having their best season in the ABL by this point in their early history as a franchise with an above-average 26–15 record for a second place finish to be behind only the defending champion Wilkes-Barre Barons for an easy qualification for the ABL Playoffs this season. After waiting for the long-standing Paterson Crescents and newly-added Bridgeport Newfield Steelers to play their fourth place tiebreaker match to determine the final spot in the ABL Playoffs, Scranton would go up against the Crescents out in nearby Paterson for the semifinal round; while the series would be surprisingly tense and close, Scranton would ultimately sweep their close rivals 2–0 to get a shot at the championship series against the defending champion Barons. After losing the first game of the series, Scranton would end up winning their next two games in the championship series to have a shot at stealing the series away from Wilkes-Barre, though the Miners would ultimately blow the series by losing their final two games to be eliminated 3–2 by the defending champions of the ABL.


Roster
Due to information on American Basketball League players being generally hard to find (especially by the time the ABL was designated more as a minor league during its later years of existence), there are bound to be more gaps and/or inaccuracies found in certain areas on the team's roster spots than usual.


American Basketball League Standings

The Paterson Crescents would defeat the Bridgeport Newfield Steelers 99–80 on March 26, 1949 in the fourth place tiebreaker match for the season, which would ensure their qualification for the 1949 ABL Playoffs for this season.

However, after having disappointing attendance results for home games at the Million Dollar Pier in Atlantic City, the team returned to Philadelphia to become the Sphas once again for the rest of the season going forward into their existence on January 16, 1949, with them playing their final home games as a professional basketball team back at their primary home court in the ballroom area of the Broadwood Hotel.

The 1948–49 Bridgeport Newfield Steelers season was officially the first professional basketball season for the team currently known as the Newfield Steelers after they previously competed in an independent manner in the 1948 World Professional Basketball Tournament (where they lost to the all-black New York Renaissance in the first round, who ended up being the runners-up for that event behind the Minneapolis Lakers). Entering this season, Bridgeport would be one of eight teams competing in the league for this season in a time where the long-standing Philadelphia SPHAs would move to Atlantic City, New Jersey to originally become the Atlantic City Tides before becoming the SPHAs once again for the rest of the season by January 1949, though the Newfield Steelers would replace the Lancaster (Red) Roses team that moved from the major ABL to the minor EPBL earlier in the previous season. Initially, Bridgeport would prove their worth regarding their promotion from being an independent team (that would apparently previously compete in the minor New England Basketball League at one point in time) to being a professional ABL team by winning their first seven games played in the season (including a game won against the similarly hot Atlantic City Tides). However, similar to the hot streak that the Tides had early on, the Newfield Steelers would end up cooling down on their play for the rest of the season, though unlike the Atlantic City Tides turned Philadelphia SPHAs, Bridgeport would end up tying with the Paterson Crescents for a fourth place finish with a 24–17 record that put the Newfield Steelers ahead of the Atlantic City Tides turned Philadelphia SPHAs, Brooklyn Gothams, and Hartford Hurricanes / Caps, but behind the defending ABL champion Trenton Tigers, the Scranton Miners, and the inaugural EPBL champion Wilkes-Barre Barons. Due to the tied fourth place finish, Bridgeport and Paterson would compete in a tiebreaker match to determine who would qualify for the final playoff spot in the ABL this season. Unfortunately for the Newfield Steelers, they would get crushed by the Crescents in the tiebreaker match with a 99–80 final score, thus eliminating the Bridgeport Newfield Steelers from their shot at competing in the ABL Playoffs properly this season.


Roster
Due to information on American Basketball League players being generally hard to find (especially by the time the ABL was designated more as a minor league during its later years of existence), there are bound to be more gaps and/or inaccuracies found in certain areas on the team's roster spots than usual.


American Basketball League Standings

The Paterson Crescents would defeat the Bridgeport Newfield Steelers 99–80 on March 26, 1949 in the fourth place tiebreaker match for the season, which would ensure their qualification for the 1949 ABL Playoffs for this season.


American Basketball League Playoffs


American Basketball League Fourth Place Tiebreaker
Before the ABL began their playoffs properly, the ABL would schedule a fourth place tiebreaker playoff match between the Paterson Crescents and the Bridgeport Newfield Steelers on March 26, 1949. Unfortunately for Bridgeport, they would end up being crushed by Paterson with a 99–80 defeat in the only game played for that series, meaning that the Crescents would win the final playoff spot for the season by default and the Newfield Steelers would have their season ended early with this essentially being their elimination from the ABL Playoffs for this season.

a  Before this season began, the Philadelphia Sphas moved from Philadelphia, Pennsylvania to Atlantic City, New Jersey to become the Atlantic City Tides in an effort to survive for the long-term. However, after having disappointing attendance results for home games at the Million Dollar Pier in Atlantic City, the team returned to Philadelphia to become the Sphas once again for the rest of the season going forward into their existence on January 16, 1949, with them playing their final home games as a professional basketball team back at their primary home court in the ballroom area of the Broadwood Hotel.

D'Shawn Michyel Schwartz (born February 15, 1999) is an American professional basketball player for the South East Melbourne Phoenix of the Australian National Basketball League (NBL). He played college basketball for the Colorado Buffaloes and George Mason Patriots.


Early life
Schwartz was born in Abilene, Texas. He attended Sand Creek High School in Colorado Springs, Colorado, where he played basketball and tennis.
As a junior with the basketball team in 2015–16, Schwartz averaged 19.9 points and 8.5 rebounds while shooting 41 percent (39-for-95) from 3-point range.
In September 2016, Schwartz committed to the University of Colorado Boulder. He was ranked as the No. 81 prospect in the nation and the No. 17 small forward by Scout.
As a senior in 2016–17, Schwartz averaged 25.7 points and 9.8 rebounds, helping the Scorpions to 19–6 record and a spot in the Sweet 16 of the Class 4A Tournament. He earned Class 4A All-State honors, was named a McDonald's All-America nominee, and received All-Colorado recognition from the Denver Post and All-USA Colorado honors. In the first game of his senior year, he scored 50 points against Thornton, setting a Sand Creek record for points scored in a game. He also holds school records for total single-season points (617), career points (1,693), career 3-pointers (327) and career rebounds (723).


College career
As a freshman with the Colorado Buffaloes in 2017–18, Schwartz averaged 3.4 points and 1.8 rebounds in 13 minutes per game. He was one of just five Buffaloes to play in all 32 games and logged four starts. He scored a season-high 10 points twice.
As a sophomore in 2018–19, Schwartz averaged 9.2 points and 3.7 rebounds while starting 35 games. He had 18 double-figure scoring games, leading the Buffaloes in scoring three times. He scored a career-high 22 points against Washington on January 12, 2019. For the conference season, he was named Pac-12 All-Academic Second Team.
As a junior in 2019–20, Schwartz averaged 9.8 points, 3.6 rebounds and 1.4 assists, starting in 31 of 32 contests while leading Colorado in 3-point field goals made (51) and attempted (139). He scored in double-digits 18 times and led the Buffaloes in scoring in five games. He had a season-high 20 points twice.
As a senior in 2020–21, Schwartz averaged 9.3 points, 4.1 rebounds and 1.3 assists over 30 games with 29 starts. He led the Buffaloes in 3-point percentage (46 of 115), with his .400 mark from beyond the 3-point arc ranking the seventh best single-season performance in team history with a minimum 100 attempts. He scored in double-figures 13 times and had a season-high 18 points twice, finishing his four-year career as the 37th Buffalo to reach the 1,000-point plateau.
After graduating from Colorado, Schwartz opted to continue his career as a graduate transfer at George Mason University with the extra year of eligibility granted to all NCAA student-athletes in 2020–21 due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
In the 2021–22 season with the George Mason Patriots, Schwartz averaged 15.5 points and 4.6 rebounds per game, including 16.8 points per game in A-10 play. He tallied nine 20+ point efforts, including a season-high 29 points against South Dakota State on November 24, 2021. His 76 3-pointers made on the year ranked seventh-most in Mason single season history. He was named to the A-10 All-Academic Team. Following the season, he competed in the NABC Reese's All-Star Game.


Professional career
After going undrafted in the 2022 NBA draft, Schwartz joined the New York Knicks for the 2022 NBA Summer League. He appeared in one Summer League game for the Knicks.
On July 30, 2022, Schwartz signed with Fenerbahçe Koleji of the Turkish Basketball First League (TBL). In 29 games in 2022–23, he averaged 17.8 points, 6.6 rebounds and 2.0 assists per game.
On June 14, 2023, Schwartz signed with Le Mans Sarthe Basket of the French LNB Élite. In 34 league games in 2023–24, he averaged 7.8 points and 3.0 rebounds per game. He also averaged 10.1 points, 3.9 rebounds and 1.0 assists in eight BCL games.
On July 30, 2024, Schwartz signed with the Rostock Seawolves of the German Basketball Bundesliga. In mid-February 2025, he suffered a season-ending pectoral muscle injury. In 19 games in 2024–25, he averaged 13.9 points, 4.7 rebounds and 1.1 assists per game.
On June 26, 2025, Schwartz re-signed with the Seawolves for the 2025–26 season. In a May 2026 game against Rasta Vechta, he recorded 39 points and 14 rebounds and made 7-of-10 3-pointers in a 12-point win. In 35 league games, he averaged 14.0 points, 6.0 rebounds and 1.3 assists per game. He also averaged 12.8 points, 5.8 rebounds and 1.5 assists in 12 FIBA Europe Cup games.
On July 21, 2026, Schwartz signed with the South East Melbourne Phoenix of the Australian National Basketball League (NBL) for the 2026–27 season.

Al Puerto de Veracruz ("To the Port of Veracruz"), also El Puerto de Veracruz, was a department store in the Historic center of Mexico City, on the corner of 5 de Febrero and Venustiano Carranza streets. It also had a store in Puebla. It was founded in 1880 by immigrants from Barcelonnette, France, and was one of the big French-owned stores of the Porfiriato, along with El Palacio de Hierro, El Puerto de Liverpool and El Correo Francés. The Mexico City building is now a La Parisina fabric store.


History
Léon (León) Signoret was born in Barcelonnette in 1857 and came to Mexico in 1878. He first worked at Las Fábricas de Francia, a Mexico City clothing and import store. In 1880 he and his brother Antoine (Antonio) founded El Puerto de Veracruz. In 1885 Signoret set up the firm Signoret, Honnorat y Cía. with Léon Honnorat, and it then ran the store. The store sold clothing, silks, fabrics, furniture, home decoration and wines.
Proal and Charpenel give 1873 as the year Signoret arrived in Mexico, not 1878, and say he left Las Fábricas de Francia to start the store, first with Antoine as Signoret Hermanos and then with Honnorat. Former employees interviewed for their book describe it as a big store that also had its own factories, selling fabrics, safes, umbrellas and a bit of everything, at retail and wholesale; the wholesale side let it supply other stores around the country. One employee who started there in the 1920s says it then belonged to Signoret, Honnorat y Cía.; by the time of the interviews it belonged to a Spitalier from Meyronnes, in the Ubaye Valley.
In the 1890s it advertised new fashions arriving "by the steamers of each month" and had a shipping service for customers in the interior of the country.
In 1889 Signoret, Honnorat y Cía. joined Thomas Braniff and two other Barcelonnette houses, J. Tron y Cía. (El Palacio de Hierro) and Ollivier y Cía. (La Ciudad de Londres), in forming the Compañía Industrial de Orizaba, a textile company. Signoret also sat on the boards of the Banco de Londres y México, the San Rafael paper mill and the Monterrey iron and steel foundry, among others.


Buildings


Mexico City
Construction of the store's building on the corner of Capuchinas and Monterilla streets (now Venustiano Carranza and 5 de Febrero) began in 1895. At the end of the 1930s it was replaced by a modern building, which reused the steel structure. The store was still open in the mid-1980s. As of 2017 the building was a La Parisina fabric store.


Puebla
The Puebla store was on the corner of 4 Poniente and 5 de Mayo streets in the historic center. Its building went up in the 1950s and was one of the first modern buildings in the city center, and one of the first with underground parking. The store sold European, American and Mexican goods to middle- and upper-class shoppers. Telediario says it was run by Spanish businessmen based in Veracruz. During the 1990s the store was known for multiple Santa Claus figures that appeared to be climbing the building. It closed toward the end of the 20th century after more than forty years, and the building then became a bazaar selling knock-off clothing, sneaker and pirated movies and music, now focusing on cellular phones and accessories named Centro Comercial 5 de Mayo.

The June 14th Revolutionary Movement (Spanish: Movimiento Revolucionario 14 de Junio), also known as the 14th of June Movement and abbreviated 14J or 1J4, was a Dominican left-wing organization founded in January 1960 to overthrow the dictatorship of Rafael Trujillo. It took its name from the landing of Dominican exiles at Constanza, Maimón and Estero Hondo on 14 June 1959, and adopted the exiles' minimum program as its own.
The movement was the largest of the clandestine groups opposing Trujillo. After a plot to kill the dictator at a cattle fair was uncovered in January 1960, its members were arrested and tortured, among them the Mirabal sisters, three of whom were murdered in November 1960. The organization came into the open as a political party after Trujillo's assassination in 1961, refused to contest the 1962 elections, and took up arms in November 1963 against the civilian junta that had overthrown President Juan Bosch. Its leader, Manolo Tavárez Justo, was captured and executed in December 1963. The movement fought on the constitutionalist side in the Dominican Civil War of 1965 and dissolved in 1968.


Background
Trujillo governed the Dominican Republic from 1930 until his assassination in 1961, and opposition activity was confined to small clandestine cells watched by the Servicio de Inteligencia Militar (SIM). On 14 June 1959, some 150 Dominican exiles belonging to the Dominican Liberation Movement (Movimiento de Liberación Dominicana, MLD) landed at Constanza, Maimón and Estero Hondo, in the north of the country, under Enrique Jiménez Moya. They had trained in Cuba with the support of Fidel Castro, whose Cuban Revolution had triumphed on 1 January 1959. The landing was crushed within days by the Dominican army and air force, and only a handful of the expeditionaries survived. Their dead were commemorated as la Raza Inmortal ("the immortal race"), and the date of the landing became the reference point for the internal resistance that followed.


Foundation
Delegates of the resistance cells met on 10 January 1960 at a rice farm belonging to Carlos Conrado Bogaert Domínguez in Boca de Mao, in the Laguna Salada district of Valverde Province, to give the scattered groups a single organization. According to the notarized record of the founding meeting, the name was proposed by Minerva Mirabal, seconded by Dulce Tejada de Álvarez, and adopted unanimously: "From today, 10 January 1960, the cellular movement that has been organizing clandestinely at national level shall take the name of Movimiento Revolucionario 14 de Junio". The first article of the movement's decalogue adopted the minimum program of the 1959 expeditionaries.
The lawyer Manolo Tavárez Justo, husband of Minerva Mirabal, was elected president. Rafael Miguel "Pipe" Faxas Canto served as general secretary and Leandro Guzmán as treasurer. The founding group included the three activist Mirabal sisters, Patria, Minerva and María Teresa, and their husbands, as well as students, professionals, seminarians, workers and peasants from across the country. The historian Roberto Cassá, who dates the formal constitution of the movement to this meeting, described it as the most significant revolutionary organization in the history of the Dominican republic; its membership was later put at about 6,000.


Repression
In January 1960 the movement planned to assassinate Trujillo at a cattle fair. The plot was discovered before it could be carried out, and the SIM arrested members across the country. Prisoners were taken to the torture centers at La 40 and at kilometer 9 of the Mella highway, where they were beaten and subjected to electric shocks. Tavárez Justo was held in La 40 and then transferred to the prison in Puerto Plata; Patria Mirabal's house, where the group had met, was burned.
Minerva and María Teresa Mirabal were released, but their husbands remained in prison in Puerto Plata, and the sisters had to travel there over the mountain road from Salcedo. On 25 November 1960 the three sisters and their driver, Rufino de la Cruz, were stopped on that road and killed by agents of the SIM. The murders, ordered by Trujillo, discredited the regime at home and abroad; Trujillo was himself assassinated on 30 May 1961.


Political party
On 8 July 1960 the movement announced that it was coming into the open and organizing as a party, and a constituent assembly with delegates from every province was held that month. After the fall of the Trujillo family, an amnesty on 26 July 1961 freed Tavárez Justo, and the organization took the name June 14 Political Group (Agrupación Política 14 de Junio). It became, with the Dominican Revolutionary Party and the National Civic Union, one of the three largest political forces of the transition, and took part in the mobilizations that forced the Trujillo family into exile.
The movement declined to contest the elections of 20 December 1962, which it considered to offer no real guarantees, and called on its supporters to abstain; other groups of the left, including the Popular Socialist Party and the Dominican Popular Movement, did the same. Juan Bosch of the Dominican Revolutionary Party won the presidency with close to 60 percent of the vote and took office in February 1963.
Relations between Bosch and the movement were uneasy. Tavárez Justo publicly warned the new president that a coup was being prepared with the backing of the church, the business elite, the high command, and the United States embassy, and criticized what he called the government's turn away from the people. On 25 September 1963 the army overthrew Bosch and installed a three-man civilian junta headed by Emilio de los Santos.


Insurrection of 1963
The junta annulled the 1963 constitution, dissolved Congress and outlawed the parties of the left. The 14J declared itself "standard-bearer of the return of the constitutional regime" in a communiqué on 22 November and, on the night of 28 November 1963, rose in arms in six guerrilla fronts named after Dominican and foreign figures of the anti-Trujillo struggle: Juan de Dios Ventura Simó, Gregorio Luperón, Mauricio Báez, Hermanas Mirabal, Francisco del Rosario Sánchez and Enrique Jiménez Moya. Tavárez Justo was commander-in-chief and placed himself at the Jiménez Moya front, in Las Manaclas, in the mountains of San José de las Matas.
The insurrection had been prepared in about two months. The guerrillas lacked training, boots and food, many of their weapons were unusable, and they found little support among the peasantry; the army bombed the mountains with P-51 Mustang aircraft, and on 3 December the junta outlawed the movement by decree. Within three weeks four of the six fronts had been destroyed. On 21 December, at a place called Alto de la Diferencia, Tavárez Justo and a group of his men surrendered after receiving assurances that their lives would be respected, and were shot. The government first described his death as a suicide and then as a death in combat; the bodies were buried in a common grave. Estimates of the total dead range from fifteen to twenty-nine.
The executions broke the junta's civilian facade. De los Santos resigned in protest on 22 December, and the breakdown of the coalition opened the way for Donald Reid Cabral, who took over the government in 1964.


Civil war and dissolution
The movement was weakened but not destroyed by the death of its leader. When part of the army rose against Reid Cabral on 24 April 1965, opening the Dominican Civil War, its provisional central committee decided to join the constitutionalist side, and its members formed many of the armed comandos that fought in Santo Domingo alongside the Dominican Revolutionary Party, the Popular Socialist Party and the Dominican Popular Movement. The extent of communist or Castroite influence among the constitutionalists, and within the 14J in particular, was disputed at the time and has been since. In October 1965, after the ceasefire, its headquarters in Santo Domingo was one of the positions stormed by the army in an operation against armed groups that had refused to disarm.
With its leader dead, its cadres imprisoned or exiled, and its role in the April revolution questioned by its own members, the movement went into crisis after 1965 and dissolved in 1968.

Thomas Braniff (1830 – January 22, 1905) was an American-born railway manager, industrialist and banker in Mexico. He came to Mexico to work on the Mexican Railway between Mexico City and Veracruz, ran it for about thirty years, and became one of the richest businessmen of the Porfiriato, with large stakes in textile mills, the San Rafael paper company and the Banco de Londres y México.


Early life
Braniff was born in 1830 on Staten Island, New York, the son of Thomas Braniff and Anna Woods, both Irish immigrants.


Railway
Braniff came to Mexico in 1863, hired to work on the construction of the Ferrocarril Mexicano (Mexican Railway) as an employee of Smith, Knight and Company. During the Second Mexican Empire he used his position to bring in luxury goods from abroad and sell them in Mexico, some of it as contraband. As superintendent engineer he also advised on the layout of the Paseo de la Emperatriz (now Paseo de la Reforma). He is said to have favored the monarchy and to have secretly accompanied Empress Carlota to Veracruz in 1866.
After the fall of the Empire, Braniff became a shareholder and then director of the company that finished the railway. He was named director in 1874 and stayed on until shortly before his death. He was also its general manager.


Business
Braniff backed the regime of Porfirio Díaz, and it was under Díaz that he made most of his fortune. Most of his money went into industry; he also invested in real estate, banking and loans, and in smaller amounts in commerce, railways, mines and haciendas.
In 1881 he built the San Lorenzo textile mill at Nogales, near Orizaba. In 1889 he was among the founding shareholders of the new Compañía Industrial de Orizaba (CIDOSA), set up with the Mexico City retailers founded by immigrants from Barcelonette, France (e.g. Al Puerto de Veracruz, El Palacio de Hierro, La Ciudad de Londres, El Puerto de Liverpool). In 1899 he and Ricardo Segura formed Segura, Braniff y Compañía, which supplied electric light to Orizaba.
In 1894 he and José Sánchez Ramos were the two founding shareholders of the San Rafael paper company; by 1899 he held 36.37% of its capital, and 22.9% in 1905. With the same partners he owned the Agrícola de Xico, which worked seven haciendas around Chalco. He led a group of Mexican investors who bought into the Banco de Londres y México.
At his death, about 45% of his invested capital was in industry, 21% in real estate, 14% in the Banco de Londres y México and 10% in loans.


Personal life
Braniff married Lorenza Ricard Werdalle (1845–1934), who was born in Biarritz; she helped him connect with Mexico City's Barcelonnette community. They had seven children: Jorge, Óscar, Tomás, Arturo, Lorenza (later Marquesa de Mohernando), Rafael and the aviator Alberto Braniff.
In 1886 Braniff hired architect Charles J. S. Hall to build a house at Paseo de la Reforma 27, on a lot of about 4,300 square metres (46,000 sq ft). It went up from 1888 in a French eclectic style and had 34 rooms and large gardens. After his death it was used during the 1910 Centenary festivities and during the Revolution. The land was sold in 1932 and the house was torn down. The Reforma 27 tower by Alberto Kalach now stands on the south part of the lot.
Braniff died on January 22, 1905, and had a lavish funeral the next day. His children had a marble monument built for him at the Panteón Francés de la Piedad by architect Augusto C. Volpi.

James "Jim" Murray McCabe is an American attorney primarily recognized for his extensive legal career serving as Associate General Counsel for the Watchtower Bible and Tract Society, the primary legal entity of Jehovah's Witnesses. Over several decades, McCabe has been a prominent legal representative for the organization, handling complex corporate, constitutional, and high-profile liability litigations across the world.


Life
McCabe grew up in the Los Angeles metropolitan area and he was raised in a Catholic household. Jim attained his undergraduate degree at the University of Washington where he attended on a football scholarship playing for the Washington Huskies in Seattle under head coach Jim Owens. McCabe was a two-year fullback and running back from 1966 through 1967, establishing himself as the starting fullback during his senior year (his final year in college).
In 1968, James M. McCabe matriculated at the University of San Diego School of Law. During his initial semesters, McCabe's academic approach was influenced by personal religious obligations, specifically attending Mass under the assumption that spiritual devotion would correlate with high academic achievement. During this first semester, he met Melinda, whom he married the following summer. Shortly after their wedding, following an instance of arriving late to a service, the couple experienced a sense of alienation from the religious community. This event marked a permanent departure from practicing the Catholic faith.
McCabe subsequently completed in 1971 his legal education at the University of San Diego School of Law, earning a Juris Doctor degree. He was admitted to the State Bar of California on January 5, 1972, initially establishing a practice in criminal law and later expanding into personal injury, trusts, and corporate matters.
In the mid-1970s, McCabe underwent a religious conversion after his wife began studying scripture with Jehovah's Witnesses. Following a period of personal theological examination, he was baptized into the faith in 1975. By June 1979, McCabe left his private partnership to enter full-time voluntary ministry for the denomination, and in September 1979, he was appointed to be an overseer.
He eventually transitioned his legal expertise to the organization's headquarters, serving as an organizational overseer and a primary architect of its legal defense frameworks. To facilitate multi-jurisdictional defense, McCabe maintained active legal licenses in multiple states, including California and New York.


Career and Controversies
McCabe's legal career is defined by his defense of the Watchtower Society in cases involving religious freedom, internal church discipline, and organizational liability.
McCabe played a key role as in-house legal counsel and Associate General Counsel for the Watchtower Bible and Tract Society. In this capacity, he traveled across various continents (including Eastern Europe, Latin America, Africa, and Asia) to legally represent the interests of religious minorities and promote freedom of worship before government agencies.
At the national level in the United States, he has led the defense strategy for the Watchtower and various local congregations in civil lawsuits. Among his most high-profile legal interventions was the 2012 litigation in the state of California involving the case of Candace Conti, a civil trial with significant international repercussions related to the religious organization's institutional protocols regarding allegations of child abuse.


First Amendment and Church Discipline
Throughout his career, McCabe defended the right of religious institutions to establish internal parameters for membership and worship, arguing that institutional autonomy over congregational access is constitutionally protected under the First Amendment.


Institutional Liability Litigation
In the 2010s, McCabe served as the lead trial attorney defending the Watchtower Society and individual congregations in landmark civil lawsuits regarding allegations of child sexual abuse within the organization (notably the Candace Conti and Fremont, California liability cases).


Public Statements and Professional Standing
In public statements and high-profile media appearances, such as on PBS NewsHour and C-SPAN, McCabe consistently maintained that the religious organization abhors child abuse. Professionally, he argued against the imposition of organizational negligence for the actions of individual rank-and-file members, actively appealing massive punitive damages levied against Watchtower entities.
McCabe remains an active member of the State Bar of California, continuing his practice out of San Diego, California, and the Watchtower facilities in Patterson, New York.

Schemetropolitan Platon (Russian: Схимитрополит Платон), secular name Vladimir Petrovich Udovenko (Russian: Владимир Петрович Удовенко; 17 November 1940 – 6 November 2026), was a bishop of the Russian Orthodox Church who served as the Metropolitan of Feodosia and Kerch (2012–2024). He also served as a People's Deputy of the RSFSR and the Russian Federation (1990–1993) and was a member of the Supreme Soviet of the RSFSR and the Russian Federation (1991–1992).
His name day is 18 November (1 December), the feast day of the Venerable Platon of Ancyra.


Biography


Before the Episcopate
Platon was born in Ukraine, the fourth child in a family that lost its father early on. After finishing school in 1957, he entered the Dormition Monastery in Odessa as a novice, where he spent a year working in the candle workshop.
In 1958, he began his studies at the Kiev Theological Seminary; after its closure in 1960, he continued his education at the Odessa Theological Seminary.
From 1961 to 1964, he served in the Soviet Army, after which he resumed his seminary studies.
In 1965, Platon graduated from the seminary and enrolled at the Leningrad Theological Academy, where he defended a thesis in theology titled "A Historical Overview of the Relations Between the Russian and Roman Catholic Churches."
He worked as an aide in the Department for External Church Relations of the Moscow Patriarchate while simultaneously pursuing postgraduate studies at the Moscow Theological Academy.
In 1971, he was tonsured a monk by Metropolitan Nikodim (Rotov), ​​Chairman of the Department for External Church Relations of the Moscow Patriarchate (DECR). That same year, he was ordained a hierodeacon and a hieromonk. In 1972, he was elevated to the rank of archimandrite by Metropolitan Nikodim and sent to Argentina.


Episcopate
On 16 December 1973, Platon was ordained Bishop of Argentina and South America (succeeding Bishop Maxim [Krokha]); the ordination was led by Patriarch Pimen.
On 6 October 1977, he was elevated to the rank of Archbishop and appointed Exarch of Central and South America (having succeeded Archbishop Nikodim Rusnak in the administration of the Exarchate).
In 1980, Platon took charge of the Sverdlovsk and Kurgan diocese, replacing the ailing Archbishop Kliment [Perestyuk], while also assuming temporary administration of the Chelyabinsk diocese. In 1983, he succeeded in opening a parish in Kamyshlov, the first such opening in the diocese in thirty years. Furthermore, from 1981 to 1986, he served as deputy chairman of the DECR.
In 1984, after the retirement of Metropolitan Ioann (Vendland), he assumed the cathedra of the Yaroslavl and Rostov Diocese. During his tenure, eight churches in the city of Yaroslavl and more than fifty throughout the region were returned to the Church. He oversaw the restoration of the Tolga and Spaso-Yakovlevsky monasteries and secured the recovery of the relics of several saints and historical figures: the princes Fyodor Rostislavich Cherny ("the Black"), David Fyodorovich, Konstantin Fyodorovich Ulemets, Vasily Vsevolodovich, and Konstantin Vsevolodovich; the hierarchs Dimitry of Rostov, Innokenty of Irkutsk, and Ignatius Brianchaninov; and the Venerable Avraamy (Abraham) of Rostov.
On 18 March 1990, Platon was elected a People's Deputy of the RSFSR from the Kirovsky territorial electoral district No. 773 (Yaroslavl). From 2 November 1991, to 14 December 1992, he served as a member of the Supreme Soviet of the RSFSR (later the Russian Federation). He worked on the Supreme Soviet’s committee on freedom of conscience and participated in drafting the law "On Freedom of Religious Beliefs." According to contemporary sociologists, this law opened the way for the formation of numerous "new religious movements."
In June 1990, he participated in the Local Council of the Russian Orthodox Church; he proposed reducing the number of votes required for a candidate – nominated by the Local Council – to be included in the final list of candidates for the office of Patriarch, but the proposal was not adopted. The Archbishop himself put forward the candidacy of Metropolitan Pitirim (Nechaev) of Volokolamsk. Furthermore, Archbishop Platon took part in various discussions, for instance, by suggesting that an address be sent to the parishioners of the Russian Orthodox Church Outside of Russia (ROCOR).
From 1 November 1993, Platon once again administered the Diocese of Argentina and South America.
In 2000, he participated in the Bishops' Council of the Russian Orthodox Church, where he spoke out against limiting the authority of the Local Council.
On 29 February 2004, Platon was elevated to the rank of metropolitan by Patriarch Alexy II.
From 2010 to 26 July 2012, he served as the second Vice-Chairman of the Assembly of Orthodox Bishops of South America.
On 26 July 2012, he was relieved of his duties as head of the Argentine-South American Diocese and transferred to the Ukrainian Orthodox Church (Moscow Patriarchate), with his subsequent assignment to a see to be determined by the Holy Synod of the latter. Before his final departure from the South American diocese, Metropolitan Platon paid a farewell visit to all the parishes in his diocese.
On 20 December 2012, Platon was appointed to head the newly established Eparchy of Feodosia and Kerch.
On 7 June 2022, at a meeting of the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church, the Feodosia Diocese was placed under the direct canonical and administrative jurisdiction of the Patriarch of Moscow and All Rus' and the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church.
On 25 July 2024, by a decision of the Holy Synod of the Russian Orthodox Church, Platon was relieved of his duties as head of the Feodosia Diocese, with an expression of gratitude for his many years of archpastoral labour, and granted retirement; the Republic of Crimea was designated as his place of residence during retirement, with his financial support to be provided by the Feodosia Diocese.
On 23 July 2026, with the blessing of His Holiness Patriarch Kirill of Moscow and All Rus', he was tonsured into the Great Schema, retaining his name but now in honour of a different saint: the Martyr Platon of Ancyra.


Death
Platon died on 6 November 2026, at the age of 85.

Sanctions
On 24 January 2023, against the backdrop of the Russian invasion of Ukraine, Platon was placed on Ukraine's sanction list, which entailed the freezing of his assets, restrictions on commercial transactions, a ban on the outflow of capital from the country, and the revocation of any Ukrainian state awards.

Daniel Ndoundou (or Danieli; 1 May 1911 – 6 January 1986) was a Congolese Protestant minister. A pastor of the Evangelical Church of Congo, he led the Christian revival of the Republic of the Congo.


Early life and education
Ndoundou was born on 1 May 1911, in Kindamba (then part of French Congo), the son of Nsemi Mboko and Bouanga Bua Mboukou. His father was not Christian, though his mother was, so he and his siblings were raised as such. He was close with his paternal uncle, evangelist Nsemi Noé.
Ndoundou was baptized on 6 June 1923, at the Kingoyi mission station, with him losing his mother the same year. Although from the Republic of the Congo, he was educated at schools in the Democratic Republic of the Congo; from 1922 to 1925, he attended school in Kimwenza, and from 1925 to 1928, attended school in Kingoyi. He then became a train dispatcher for the Matadi–Kinshasa Railway. From 1929 to 1930, he studied at the Mukimbungu Bible School.


Religious career
Ndoundou worked a well-paying job in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, though, in 1931, he returned to the Republic of the Congo. He then became a traveling evangelist for the Swedish Evangelical Mission in Ngouédi. From 1933 to 1936, he studied at the Seminary of Ngouédi, afterwards working as an evangelist for the village. From 1941 to 1943, he and his wife, studied at the School of Pastors and Teachers, in Kimpese.
On 16 June 1946, Ndoundou was ordained a pastor into the Evangelical Church of Congo (ECC), in Dolisie. In 1947, Ngouédi and the surrounding region was amid a religious drought. The prayers of student Raymond Buana Kibongui and pastor John Magnusson brought religion back to the community. Kibongui left on scholarship and Ndoundou became a figure in the revival. From 1947 to 1965, he was a member of the Synodal Council, which led a Christian revival in the Republic of the Congo. He was a widely-visited faith healer among the Congolese, reportedly curing medical problems through prayer.
From 1950 to 1974, Ndoundou hosted biannual religious conferences in Kindamba. In 1963, 260,000 attended the conference, including President Fulbert Youlou. He was a spiritual advisor to President Alphonse Massamba-Débat.
In 1960, Ndoundou was made the national pastor-evangelist of the Evangelical Church of Congo (ECC), which he remained as until his retirement. He supported independence of the Congolese church and government. Upon the independence of the ECC from the Swedish Church, in 1961, he was made its vice-president, which he was until 1965. In 1963, he was awarded the Order of Congolese Merit. In 1970, he and his family moved to Loutété. He acted as a mediator for interpersonal and interethnic conflicts, which earned him the nickname "Vukamakanda". In 1970, he visited churches in Scandinavia.
Ndoundou often had theophanies in his dreams which guided his religious work. As a preacher, he often employed slogans, including "Sala-sambila, sambila-sala" (work-pray, pray-work) and "sikama, siama, tatamana, ndungunu" (Wake up, take courage, persevere, victory). He believed that the ECC never schism. He was inspired by Simon Kimbangu.


Personal life and death
Ndoundou was married to Charlotte Nsouka; together they had two children, with her dying in 1937, during the birth of their second child. In 1941, he married Nsungi Mickaelle. She died in 1951, and in 1954, he married Henriette Mpombo, a primary school teacher. He retired in 1984. Also in 1984, he suffered a stroke due to high blood pressure. In 1985, he was admitted to the Montfermeil Hospital. He died on 6 January 1986, aged 74, in Montfermeil.


References

Gaatske Adriana "Adri" Ladenius (29 October 1883 – 21 April 1953), also known as Adri Leendertz-Ladenius, was a Dutch politician for the Social Democratic Workers' Party (SDAP), campaigner for women's suffrage, painter and illustrator of children's books.  In 1919, she became the first woman on the Almelo municipal council and in the Provincial Council of Overijssel.


Early life and education
She was born in Emmen as the third of seven children of Hendrik Johannes Ladenius (1854–1925), a clerk at the subdistrict court (kantongerecht) in Emmen who later became a subdistrict judge in Almelo, and Catharina Elisabeth Beucker Andreae (1853–1950). She was named after her maternal grandmother, Gaatske Adriana Haverschmidt, a sister of the minister and poet François Haverschmidt, who wrote as Piet Paaltjens.
The family was strictly Dutch Reformed and lived in Zuidhorn and Vianen; in Utrecht she attended a Christian girls' school for some time. In 1897 the family moved to Almelo, where she went to the Hogere Burgerschool. Afterwards she spent a year abroad. In 1902 she took lessons at the Dagtekenschool voor meisjes (day drawing school for girls) in Amsterdam, and then studied painting until 1907 at the Academie voor Beeldende Kunsten (academy of fine arts). In 1907 she returned to Almelo.


Social Democratic Workers' Party
Ladenius came to sympathise with socialism, read Het Volk and in 1908 joined the SDAP in Almelo. In the summer of 1909 she became secretary of the local branch, in 1911 its treasurer, and in 1916 secretary of the party's Overijssel region. She spoke at public meetings and set up a women's club. For many years she was also active in the Bond van Sociaal-Democratische Vrouwenclubs (League of Social Democratic Women's Clubs).
During the so-called Tribune affair she signed, with Henri Polak, Carry Pothuis-Smit and Roosje Vos among others, the manifesto of the "48 Marxists". It condemned the stance of the group around the party newspaper De Tribune but also opposed an extraordinary party congress. When the Communist wing split from the SDAP in 1909, she kept her distance from it. In 1913 she was among those opposed to the SDAP taking part in government.
Ladenius wanted the vote for all women and opposed dameskiesrecht ("ladies' suffrage"). She preferred to wait longer for women's suffrage than risk a disguised form of ladies' suffrage being introduced by a liberal-socialist cabinet. For the suffrage movement she drew a series of prints, and she regularly supplied illustrations to De Proletarische Vrouw (The Proletarian Woman), for instance around International Women's Day in 1916. In 1918 she published the pamphlet In den spoortrein. Het belang van de arbeidersvrouw bij de gemeenteraadspolitiek (On the train: the working-class woman's interest in municipal politics). In it, women on a train discuss their worries, such as poverty, poor health care and war, and conclude that the vote for working-class women would be a good thing.
Around 1916 she joined the Nederlandsche Vereeniging tot Afschaffing van Alcoholhoudende Dranken (Dutch Association for the Abolition of Alcoholic Drinks); in 1941 the association marked her 25 years of membership. In the 1918 Dutch general election she was a candidate for the SDAP, in an unelectable position on the list. At the time women could be elected but could not yet vote themselves.


Municipal and provincial councils
In 1919 Ladenius became the first woman on the Almelo municipal council, of which she remained a member until 1941. In the same year she was elected in the provincial elections as the first female member of the Provincial Council of Overijssel. She was thus among the first women in the Netherlands to sit on a provincial council. After her election she gave up her positions on the branch committee and as regional secretary. She remained a member of the regional executive and joined the party council.
On the municipal council she argued for public and secondary education. The council appointed her to the local committee that supervised the secondary schools, and to the board of the Almelo vocational school for girls.
As a member of the Provincial Council she concentrated on public health, social provision and popular education. She made her first speech under any other business (rondvraag) at the meeting of 16 December 1919. In it she asked the provincial executive to investigate how the sick were nursed, in particular people with tuberculosis. Her proposal to found a provincial sanatorium for patients with lung disease brought her into conflict with the Anti-Revolutionary member of the provincial executive Hendrik van der Vegte. He did not see it as a task for the province and preferred a Christian sanatorium founded by private initiative. She paid particular attention to public library provision: she saw the reading of good novels as a remedy for the boredom she noticed above all in the countryside.
Twice she was called to order by the Queen's commissioner A.F.L. van Rechteren Limpurg Almelo. She did not hesitate to interrupt him when she thought it necessary. With his successors she generally got on well. She was always nervous before her speeches in the council, because, as the only woman on the council, she did not want to fail. Until 1948 she had no female colleague on the Provincial Council of Overijssel; in that year the communist J. Broekman-Smit joined her. Her membership was interrupted by the German occupation; she remained a member of the Provincial Council until 1950.
In 1946, as the council's oldest member, she welcomed the new commissioner, J.B.G.M. de van der Schueren. In the same year she was made a Knight of the Order of Orange-Nassau. When Queen Wilhelmina marked her jubilee as monarch in 1948, Ladenius addressed the council. She looked back on half a century in which two world wars had taken place and workers and women had won the vote. Women were not yet well represented in government, parliament, provincial councils and municipal councils, she remarked, "maar misschien komt dat later nog" ("but perhaps that will come later").


Drawings
Alongside her political work Ladenius illustrated children's books. In 2026 the Rijksmuseum held drawings by her, among them a design for a book illustration from a gift by the publisher L.J. Veen.


Personal life and death
In Almelo on 1 November 1921 Ladenius married Albertus Cornelis Leendertz (1881–1953), a judge who later became president of the Almelo district court. That he held different political views did not hinder her political activities. The couple had no children.
Her funeral in Almelo was simple but well attended, both by workers and working-class women and by people from her own social circle. Her brother-in-law W. Leendertz, a professor at the Mennonite seminary, led the service.


Commemoration
In 1946 she was made a Knight of the Order of Orange-Nassau.
In Almelo the G.A. Leendertz-Ladeniuslaan bears her name, and in 1986 the Ladeniusmarke in Zwolle-Zuid was named in her honour.

The House of Lysias is the largest residential building in Dura Europos. It was the residence of the local governors, the so-called Strategoi, for several generations. Dura Europos is a well-preserved ancient city in present-day Syria. The place was a Hellenistic foundation, then conquered by the Parthians, and eventually came under Roman rule. In the year 266 AD, Dura Europos was conquered by the Sassanids. Since the city was never resettled after that, it is still relatively well preserved today.
The house occupies an entire insula (modern number D1).  The name Lysias was found several times in the house. From other sources, a governor with the full name Septimius Aurelius Lysisa is known, who was likely the resident of the house. His family held high offices in the city from the time of the Arsacids (Parthians) to the time of the Romans. The house was located on the main street of the city and directly opposite the Temple of Zeus Megistos.  In and around the temple, there are inscriptions that clearly demonstrate the close connection of the Lysias family with the temple. 
The house was uncovered between 1935 and 1936 through excavations by the American Yale University. A French excavation expedition, which worked in the city before 2011, uncovered the building again. 
The entire building is arranged around a courtyard in the northern section and features various special rooms that are not found in other houses in the city. Apparently, the insula was originally inhabited by several residential houses, but the owners of the house seem to have gradually acquired the neighboring houses and integrated them into the larger whole.  In the courtyard, there were columns that possibly belonged to a loggia on the south side of the courtyard. This could have provided access to rooms on the upper floor. The building had several staircases, but it is uncertain whether there was more than one additional floor. The courtyards of the house were paved with fired bricks, while the majority of the structure was made of unfired bricks. In the courtyards, there were altars. During the excavations, the researchers came across remnants of figurative wall paintings, and some rooms featured decorated stucco cornices as the upper wall finish.  Fragments of sculptures were also found in the house, which in turn underscore the wealth of the former inhabitants. 
Different rooms had economic functions. In many cases, these are no longer recognizable, but there is clear evidence of food storage and meal preparation. A room seems to have served as a bath, although running water is not attested. 
The walls of the building were adorned with numerous graffiti, including some with the name Lysias and dates. Since the years are too far apart to be attributed to a single person, there must have been several men with this name. The Lysias family is known through numerous inscriptions in the city, in which members bear the title strategos – governor. Notably, this title is never mentioned in the inscriptions of the house.

St Luke's Church is a heritage-listed Anglican church and associated churchyard at Little Akaloa on Banks Peninsula, in the Canterbury Region of New Zealand. Built during 1905–1906, the building has a strong connection with English-born John Henry Menzies, who not only designed and built it but also financed its construction. The church was registered by the New Zealand Historic Places Trust as a Historic Place Category I in 1993 and is notable for its extensive usage of Māori art forms, carved by Menzies, in its interior.


Background
The first European settlers on Banks Peninsula, in the Canterbury Region of New Zealand, began arriving from the 1840s. Little Akaloa was one of many bays in which there was a small farming community. In 1856 the Bishop of New Zealand George Selwyn held the first service in the community under a tree and a few years later a small, one-room school building was erected in which services were held on Sundays. In due course this was extended and in 1882 it became a dedicated church, consecrated as St Luke's. However, this was still not fit for purpose and the building was not well maintained.


History
John Henry Menzies was a English immigrant to New Zealand, who took up farming in Little Akaloa in 1877. In 1904 he offered £400 towards the construction of a new church, on the basis the local residents would contribute another £200. They swiftly raised more than needed, over £300. However, when the construction was put up for tender, it transpired the total amount was insufficient and Menzies took it upon himself to increase his financial commitment. He also undertook to carry out its construction, which commenced in 1905. The local community also contributed with free labour and cartage.
The new St Luke's Church was consecrated on 17 December 1906, with the Bishop of Christchurch Churchill Julius leading the well-attended service. At the time, its cost of construction was reported as being £1,100. Damaged in an earthquake in December 1922, work was done on the building for sake of structural reinforcement. It underwent a restoration in 1955, the completion of which was recognised with a service held by the Bishop of Christchurch, Reverend A. H. Warren.


Design
St Luke's Church and its churchyard, with access through a lychgate, is positioned on a rise overlooking Little Akaloa Bay. Designed in the Gothic Revival style, it is constructed largely of un-reinforced concrete, with shingle from a nearby beach carted to the site. Pāua shell was applied to the exterior concrete coating as it was setting. Relatively large given the small size of the Little Akaloa community, the building has a substantially cruciform floor plan; in addition to the nave, the building has transepts, a vestry wing, and an entrance porch. A belfry tower, with a bellcote capped with a pyramidal spire, is provided to the south side of the building. The gable roof was originally clad with tōtara shingles but these have been replaced with slate tiles.
The outline of the Gothic-arched door and windows of the church are defined by stone dressings. Four sets of lancet windows are provided to each end of the building, and these include Scottish-sourced leadlights arranged in geometric patterns. 
In the interior, the walls are lined with Oamaru stone and of limestone, sourced from Mount Somers. The altar, pulpit, and font, which was donated by Menzies's wife in memory of the couple's deceased children, are also of limestone, as is the communion rail. The roof space is lined mostly with rimu timber. 

Unusually for a European-designed church and congregation, this includes extensive Māori art forms. These were carved by Menzies, who was keenly interested in Māori art. Holding what was then a widespread belief that the Māori population was in decline, he saw his work as helping to preserve the art for future generations. Consequently, the pilasters, corbels and cornices are all carved with traditional Māori motifs while the roof space includes kōwhaiwhai painting. The church furniture receives similar treatment; for example, the font includes nīkau palm fronds at its base in addition to Māori and Celtic motifs while the lectern includes Māori patterns.


Legacy
St Luke's Church remains in use as part of the Community Church of Akaroa Banks Peninsula of the Anglican Diocese of Christchurch.
Located on Chorlton Road in Little Akaloa, St Luke's Church was listed by the Historic Places Trust as a Historic Place Category 1 on 17 December 1993. A factor in its heritage listing is its usage of Māori decorative elements that were designed by a European settler for a religious structure primarily intended for use by European settlers.

Burgess, Linda (2015). Historic Churches: A Guide to over 60 Early New Zealand Churches. Auckland: Penguin Random House New Zealand. ISBN 978-1-77553-733-5.
