Atsushi Sato (佐藤 篤司, Satō Atsushi; born 1964) is a Japanese computer graphics (CG) animator. He has maintained a long career at major Hollywood visual effects and animation studios, including Walt Disney Feature Animation, Weta Digital, and Sony Pictures Imageworks. His credits span creature and character animation on landmark films such as Dinosaur (2000), The Lord of the Rings: The Two Towers (2002) and The Return of the King (2003), I, Robot (2004), King Kong (2005), multiple Spider-Man films, and recent Japanese productions including contributions to Shin Godzilla (2016) and Godzilla Minus Zero (2026).


Life and career
Sato was born in Tokyo in 1964. He first encountered computer graphics in high school after seeing Tron (1982). He later attended the Visual Design Department of Tama Art Junior College, which later became part of Tama Art University. Part-time lecturer Etsuo Genda introduced him to early computer graphics (CG) paint tools and image-processing concepts. After graduation he worked in graphic design while continuing night classes. He also studied for roughly three years (including one year as a research student) at Tokai University's Takanawa College Computer Image Design course, where he created his own programs and images under instructors including Genda, the late Tomohiro Ohira, Masahiko Inakage, Hiroko Uchiyama, and Toshihiro Imama. At the time, formal CG education in Japan was limited to a handful of specialist schools and the earliest university programs; the curriculum was still being developed through trial and error.
While working in Japan he created full-CG imagery for local amusement-park rides and exhibition equipment using early general-purpose tools such as TDI Explorer (he has described himself as belonging to the first generation that used such tools rather than specialized software like Links or JCGL). He participated in the development of the first PlayStation demo Fortezza, was involved with a CG artist collective known as Digital Image, and won a prize in a PIXEL magazine CG contest. The contest brought him to the attention of NHK producer Hirofumi Ito, producer of The Universe Within, a pioneering CG-animated television special. This connection later enabled his move to overseas.
Sato moved to the United States in 1991. In interviews he has said he had no rigid long-term plan at the time; he intended to train in the U.S. for roughly five years and then return to Japan. He was influenced by a lifelong interest in film and a vague ambition to work on Hollywood movies (initially hoping for a place at Industrial Light & Magic). After arriving he approached Ito at a lecture, submitted a demo reel, and was directed toward Magic Box Productions, a small Japanese-American CG company in the Los Angeles area that aimed to adapt Japanese characters and properties for the international market.
He quit his Japanese job, waited several months, then flew to Los Angeles and called the company from outside its doors, insisting on a chance. He was given a three-month trial during which he single-handedly produced a short titled The Hanging Gardens of Babylon. The piece was accepted into the SIGGRAPH's Electronic Theater, securing him full employment. For approximately three years at Magic Box he worked as a generalist CG designer, creating pilot films that attempted to translate Japanese comic characters (including works inspired by Go Nagai's Mazinger, Osamu Tezuka's Astro Boy, and Shigeru Mizuki) into CG for Hollywood pitches. None of these projects succeeded commercially. He was also dispatched for three months as the sole CG artist to Francis Ford Coppola's American Zoetrope development team working on an unproduced Pinocchio project at Coppola's winery. Sato has described the experience as dreamlike, working alongside figures such as former Disney character designer Thom Enriquez and future Dinosaur (2000) co-director Eric Leighton, although the film was never made.
In the mid-1990s Sato submitted demo reels to several studios. Disney responded first around January 1996, initially offering registration in a talent system and later a role. He joined Walt Disney Feature Animation after declining a Mulan assignment that would have required relocation to Florida; the Los Angeles opportunity that followed was Dinosaur (2000). He underwent an intensive five-month training program (approximately three months of traditional animation fundamentals plus two months focused on CG), which included life drawing, analysis of Disney-style movement, and practical CG animation. He has recalled that the training was thorough and that active Disney artists served as instructors.
On Dinosaur he served as supervising character animator responsible for the herd characters, carnotaurs, and raptors. The production was large (hundreds of staff, with a dedicated facility known as The Secret Lab) and combined CG characters with live-action-style environments. He also worked as a lead animator on 102 Dalmatians (2000) and is credited in connection with other early-2000s projects. In later interviews he has discussed the distinctive "Disney style" of animation—exaggerated anticipations, follow-throughs, and expressive arcs that American animators are often trained in from the beginning—and the necessity of deliberately "switching heads" when moving to more realistic live-action creature or character work. He has noted that while the training is valuable for learning fundamentals, over-reliance on it can limit range.
While still at Disney, Sato received a recommendation from a visual-effects supervisor and a call from a Weta producer inviting him to help with the final push on The Lord of the Rings: The Two Towers (2002). He joined within roughly two weeks on an initial four-month (later extended) contract that ultimately lasted about 3.5–4 years. He worked as a senior animator on The Two Towers and The Return of the King (2003), contributing to creature performances including dragons, eagles, and other fantastical beings; he has described using sketches extensively for poses (for example, an eagle rescue shot). He later advanced to animation supervisor on I, Robot (2004) and King Kong (2005).
On King Kong he oversaw a large team; he has described the role as involving more managerial, scheduling, and political pressure than pure creative work, with the supervisor often caught between the animation team and upper management during tight deadlines. At Weta he was treated as a freelancer (individual business owner) rather than a unionized studio employee; the environment was described as "wild" compared with Disney or later Sony, with skilled freelancers gathered for intense crunches and relatively little formal overtime management. He has noted that direct conversations with Peter Jackson were possible at Weta, unlike the more layered review processes at larger Hollywood studios. After King Kong he declined further supervisory offers and returned to a senior animator role, citing a desire for broader animation experience beyond creatures, the need to improve English communication skills for leadership positions, and a growing interest in creating his own original work.
Sato joined Sony Pictures Imageworks around 2006 (beginning around Beowulf) and has remained affiliated with the studio for roughly two decades, later relocating with its Vancouver operations in 2014. He has worked primarily as a senior or character animator on both live-action visual-effects films and fully animated features. Other notable Hollywood credits include Cloudy with a Chance of Meatballs (2009), Alice in Wonderland (2010) and its sequel (2016), The Smurfs (2011), The Amazing Spider-Man (2012) and its sequel (2014), Oz the Great and Powerful (2013), Edge of Tomorrow (2014), Kingsman: The Golden Circle (2017), The Meg (2018), Spider-Man: Into the Spider-Verse (2018), Spider-Man: Far from Home (2019), The Sea Beast (2022), Ghostbusters: Frozen Empire (2024), A Minecraft Movie (2025), and KPop Demon Hunters (2025).
In a 2013 MyNavi interview conducted while he was working on Oz the Great and Powerful, Sato described Sony Pictures Imageworks' role as the primary VFX vendor handling more than 80% of the film's visuals. The animation team grew from roughly 20 to nearly 60 people, with total creative staff (including effects, compositing, and modeling) reaching 200–300 over about a year of production. He personally worked on the film for approximately six months after finishing The Amazing Spider-Man. He animated the baboons (hihi), with the direction that they should appear neither fully human nor purely wild but possess a degree of intelligence in their gestures and expressions. He noted that director Sam Raimi preferred to review rough animation in 3D from early stages, which increased rendering times and file sizes dramatically (single files reaching around 300 GB).
On Spider-Man: Into the Spider-Verse he has spoken positively about working with limited frame rates (including 2s-style animation) and drawing inspiration from Japanese anime such as Samurai Champloo. He has described the 2s approach as enjoyable because it raises the value of each individual frame and was relatively rare in U.S. production at the time. In the 2026 interviews he reflected on the hierarchical structure of Hollywood animation departments, the intense review process (daily or near-daily in-person or online reviews that can run from morning to night), and the ongoing technical challenge of photorealistic human animation versus creature work.
Sato has periodically contributed to Japanese projects as well. For Shin Godzilla (2016) he took roughly three months of leave from Imageworks, living in a weekly mansion in Setagaya while working at Shirogumi's Sangenjaya studio. He handled previs revisions and animation, including transformation sequences up to "Kamata-kun", with later follow-up from Vancouver. In 2026 he was brought back from semi-retirement by director Takashi Yamazaki to contribute key animation to Godzilla Minus Zero, particularly sequences featuring physical interaction between Godzilla and King Ghidorah. Yamazaki has specifically credited Sato's work on the large-scale kaiju combat. At Shirogumi's Chofu studio he has worked as essentially the only full-time dedicated animator amid a larger generalist team; he has remarked on the much smaller team sizes and closer director proximity compared with Hollywood (for example, sitting adjacent to Yamazaki).

Atsushi Sato at IMDb

DigitalMint, which since late 2026 has also operated as Chaintrax Cyber, is an American ransomware-payment and cryptocurrency company in Chicago whose negotiators were convicted in 2026 of conspiring with the BlackCat (ALPHV) ransomware gang against the company's own clients. It is a trade name of Red Leaf Chicago, LLC, which negotiates and makes ransom payments on behalf of ransomware victims, and offers financing for them. Founded in 2014 by Marc Grens and Jonathan Solomon, the company built and operated one of the largest bitcoin ATM networks in the United States — a network Grens later said the company knew, by 2016, was used overwhelmingly by fraud victims; it eventually closed the network in 2024. Since 2017 it has both negotiated with ransomware gangs and also bought and transmitted cryptocurrency used to make ransom payments, charging fees that, according to its California disclosure statement, may be determined by "the size of the settlement payment" and may include a "commission, spread, markup, or other compensation."
In 2026 former DigitalMint managing director of cyber incident response Angelo Martino admitted selling the secret negotiating positions and insurance limits of the firm's clients to the BlackCat attackers he was supposedly negotiating against, in what The Insurer described as "corrupting negotiations to extort $75 million"; the company's clients in five charged cases paid about $75 million in ransoms, and DigitalMint, where "only three executive founders" were "authorized to initiate wire transfers or cryptocurrency transactions," processed the payments. A second DigitalMint negotiator, Kevin Tyler Martin, deployed BlackCat ransomware with Martino and a third man. Martino was sentenced to nearly six years in prison and Martin to four. DigitalMint has said its internal controls "did not detect any anomalous behavior" in Martino's cases. It has repeatedly declined to say whether it refunded fees it charged for processing payments on the corrupted negotiations; its Terms of Service say that "all fees are non-refundable, unless otherwise explicitly agreed to in writing." A victim's lawyer told the court that neither the defendants "nor their employers" had offered to repay "one dime."
In 2026 the company began operating as Chaintrax Cyber. The Chicago Sun-Times reported in October 2026 that DigitalMint "looks to have relaunched" as Chaintrax Cyber "in the wake of a scandal involving two former employees," with the same executives. Red Leaf Chicago registered the assumed name "Chaintrax" with the Illinois Secretary of State the day after Martino was sentenced. In October 2026, after the arrest of another prominent ransomware negotiator at a separate firm, technology publications cited the DigitalMint case as the precedent for criminal charges against ransomware negotiators.


Bitcoin ATM network and fraud
Red Leaf Chicago, LLC was organized in Illinois on October 13, 2014, and registered the assumed name "Digital Mint" in December 2015. Grens has described DigitalMint as "one of the biggest players" and "one of the first movers" in the cryptocurrency ATM industry; at its peak the network comprised roughly 1,400 bitcoin ATM and teller locations in 35 states. Of that industry, Grens has said: "The players, executive founders, just we're kind of in on it on how dirty the industry is, allowing scam victimization."
The network became a channel for fraud. A January 2025 Chicago Sun-Times investigation described the city's cryptocurrency ATMs as "magnets for drug-dealing and scams on older adults." Grens told the Sun-Times that DigitalMint had discovered by 2016 that most of its customers making large purchases were scam victims, and told Wisconsin Watch that an internal investigation had found nearly all customers making transactions of $1,000 or more were victims of fraud; DigitalMint shut down the kiosks eight years later, in 2024. Grens said of the decision, "The moral of the story is, if you do the right thing this industry wouldn't exist," and "I don't want to build something dirty."


Ransom payments and fees, conflict of interest
DigitalMint began negotiating with ransomware groups and facilitating ransom payments in 2017. Unlike negotiators that only advise victims, DigitalMint both negotiates with the attackers and executes the payments: CNBC reported in 2021 that the company could complete a ransom payment within "30 to 60 minutes from initial contact," and the company said it had facilitated more than $100 million in ransomware settlements since January 2020, with a median payment of $800,000. By 2025 it said it had conducted more than 2,000 ransomware negotiations. Red Leaf Chicago is registered with the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network as a money services business under the DigitalMint name.
DigitalMint's fees are set by contract with each client. Its California Digital Financial Asset Disclosure Statement, published on digitalmint.io under California Financial Code section 3501, states that "fees may be determined by a combination of factors such as the size of the settlement payment, the virtual currency required, liquidity or any financing or pre-funding payments," and that "DigitalMint may include its commission, spread, markup, or other compensation in the price." Its Terms of Service, which apply to every Red Leaf Chicago business alias, provide that "all fees are non-refundable, unless otherwise explicitly agreed to in writing by the Company." The company retains a percentage of each ransom it transmits as a commission.
In January 2021 Grens advised ransomware victims who hire cyber incident response firms to "ensure any conflicts of interest are mitigated or fully disclosed," and to "keep their services separate from a cryptocurrency ransomware settlement provider"; in April 2026 DigitalMint described itself as handling "incident response, ransomware negotiation, and cryptocurrency settlement in one place."
Critics in the industry have argued that combining negotiation with payment for a fee tied to the ransom creates a conflict of interest. Bill Siegel, chief executive of the rival firm Coveware, said that "if an intermediary earns a large fixed percentage of a ransom, objective advice is not going to follow." Mark Camillo, chief executive of the insurer-backed group CyberAcuView, said that "many of the ransomware payers charge a percentage of the final payment which may or may not be disclosed. This makes no sense if they are also the negotiator." The company has pushed back, saying it "firmly reject[s] the notion that our business model creates misaligned incentives."


Insider extortion scheme against DigitalMint clients


Martino: selling clients' insurance limits to BlackCat
According to his plea agreement, while negotiating for five DigitalMint clients attacked by BlackCat in 2023 — a nonprofit, a financial-services company, a hospitality company, a retailer and a medical company — Martino passed their confidential negotiating strategies and insurance limits to the attackers, who paid him for the information. In one exchange he told the attackers to "keep denying our offers and i will let you know once i find out the max the[y] want to pay"; a $1 million offer became a payment of nearly $16.5 million. The five victims paid ransoms totaling about $75.3 million, including individual payments of $26.8 million and $25.7 million, all of them transmitted through DigitalMint, where by the company's account "only three executive founders" could initiate cryptocurrency transactions. Court documents also accused Martino of conducting at least 10 ransomware attacks with Martin and Goldberg, and the Sun-Times later reported that his role in the scheme was to identify victims, extract data from their computers and lock their networks.
On April 3, 2025, federal agents searched Martino's Florida home and the Department of Justice notified DigitalMint that he was under investigation. The company has given differing accounts of what it did next: it told CyberScoop that it suspended his access that day and fired him the next; it told The Record that it fired him in June; and Grens told Bloomberg News in July 2025 that the employee had been "immediately terminated." Bloomberg also reported that some law firms and insurers had warned clients against hiring DigitalMint, and quoted Grens saying the company was "not the target" of the investigation.
Martino was charged by information in February 2026 and released on bond on condition that he do no work in the cybersecurity industry. He pleaded guilty on April 14, 2026, to conspiracy to interfere with commerce by extortion. On July 9, 2026, he was sentenced to 70 months in prison; law enforcement had seized about $10 million of his assets, and Bloomberg later reported that court records showed he had used the proceeds to buy two Florida homes, a boat and vehicles. A hearing to determine restitution, originally set for September 17, was rescheduled to November 12, 2026.


Martin and Goldberg
In October 2025 a federal grand jury in Miami indicted a second DigitalMint negotiator, Kevin Tyler Martin, and Ryan Clifford Goldberg, an incident-response manager at the Israeli security firm Sygnia, on charges that between April and November 2023 they had deployed BlackCat ransomware against at least five U.S. companies and extracted about $1.2 million from a Florida medical-device firm; the indictment, returned under seal on October 2, was made public in early November 2025. According to the Justice Department, DigitalMint hired Martin as Martino's co-worker after the conspiracy had begun. The company later said both men had "preexisting relationships relating to their involvement in ransomware-related schemes before joining DigitalMint" — stating the two negotiators were already engaged in ransomware conspiracies when DigitalMint hired them. After Martino's sentencing the company said it had "maintained controls consistent with industry standards, including background checks and compliance procedures." DigitalMint said Martin had been "acting completely outside the scope of his employment" and claimed it was "a cooperating witness in the investigation and not an investigative target." Martin and Goldberg pleaded guilty and were each sentenced to four years in prison on May 1, 2026.


Refunds to victims
DigitalMint's Terms of Service make all fees non-refundable unless the company agrees otherwise in writing. Processing more than $75 million in ransom payments would have earned DigitalMint, even at modest rates for such cryptocurrency payment execution, at least $2 million in fees and commissions on the five rigged negotiations. Asked in March and July 2026 whether it had refunded the fees charged to its victimized clients, and asked by The Insurer whether it had compensated clients whose payments were inflated, DigitalMint said it was "not able to discuss specific client relationships or fee arrangements due to confidentiality obligations." In a victim impact statement filed in April 2026, counsel for the Florida medical-device company that paid a $1.2 million ransom wrote that the company had "retained supposedly reputable companies to negotiate with the ransomware attackers" only to find that "those companies employed the very Defendants who were holding the company hostage," put its total losses at "closer to $10 million," and said that "neither the Defendants nor their employers ... have offered to repay one dime."


Company response
DigitalMint said Martino's conduct had been concealed from it and that it "condemns these individuals' criminal behavior." It said "no organization can completely eliminate insider risk" and that its "internal controls did not detect any anomalous behavior in Martino's negotiations." It said that its payment and negotiation businesses "were kept rigorously compartmentalized." The company said afterward it had begun requiring that all negotiations be conducted on cloud platforms that can be audited and logged, and that chief operating officer Don Wyper would now personally oversee every engagement.


Rebranding as Chaintrax Cyber after the convictions
On October 10, 2026, the Chicago Sun-Times reported that DigitalMint's parent "seems to have started another business" as Chaintrax Cyber "in the wake of a scandal involving two former employees," offering the same services under the same executive team; it was "unclear if DigitalMint has plans to rebrand as Chaintrax," and the companies did not respond to requests for comment. A crisis-communications consultant quoted in the story compared the name change to MiniScribe, a disk-drive maker whose fraud became notorious: "MiniScribe is still known here in Colorado as the company that shipped bricks. ... The new name changed the sign on the building. It doesn't immediately change what shows up when people search and it doesn't erase the mental association for customers who were burned."
On July 10, 2026, the day after Martino was sentenced, Red Leaf Chicago registered the assumed name "Chaintrax" with the Illinois Secretary of State. On August 27 — noted by the Sun-Times as "less than two months after" final sentencing — it filed three intent-to-use trademark applications for CHAINTRAX CYBER covering "the negotiation, transaction, and settlement of cryptocurrency payments to resolve ransomware incidents"; it registered "Chaintrax Cyber" on September 2.
The Chaintrax Cyber website describes "12 years of operational experience," identifies the business as a trade name of Red Leaf Chicago, and lists the FinCEN and NMLS registration numbers belonging to DigitalMint. The company's LinkedIn page gives Chaintrax Cyber's founding year as 2026; a podcast episode published September 1 introduced Grens as "President of Chaintrax" and said he had "led Chaintrax for twelve years."


Industry scrutiny
On October 8, 2026, the FBI arrested a prominent Canadian ransomware negotiator and former incident-response executive on extortion charges that news organizations linked to the Bureau's investigation of the ShinyHunters hacking group. Coverage of the arrest cited the DigitalMint case as the precedent. Gizmodo distinguished the charges, writing that "the closer parallel is Angelo Martino, a former DigitalMint ransomware negotiator," whose conviction rested on "the same Hobbs Act statute cited in [the] complaint"; SecureWorld described the DigitalMint precedent as the first criminal case against the ransomware-negotiation industry and the October 8 case as "the second case in a year"; and Dark Reading noted that the man arrested "is not the first ransomware negotiator charged in connection with his work."
Two further federal cases against ransomware-response firms followed. In October 2026, the owner of MonsterCloud, a Florida ransomware-remediation company, pleaded not guilty in the Eastern District of New York to wire-fraud charges alleging that the firm had secretly paid ransoms while billing clients more than $19 million for recovery services. In November 2026, the co-founder of another Florida firm, RansomwareHelp, was indicted in Rhode Island on wire-fraud charges after an FBI sting in which agents posed as both a victim company and a fictitious ransomware group; Krebs on Security called the case "the latest in a string of recent prosecutions" and listed it alongside Martino's conviction. Security Point Break wrote that five people from the ransomware-response business had been charged in about a year and that "the DigitalMint case shows where these prosecutions can land."

Abner I. Benyon was an American Down Easter launched in 1874. She spent a decade traveling between the Pacific and Europe until she was sold in 1884 and renamed Alfred Watts. In October 1887, she capsized in a hurricane while in the Atlantic. All 27 onboard were washed overboard, and 25 died while in the water due to various causes. Two crew members survived, made their way back to the partially sunken vessel, and were rescued a month later. 


History
Abner I. Benyon was a three-deck ship built by Samuel Watts at Thomaston, Maine, for his own account and was launched in September 1874. She was named after the president of a bank in Boston with which many Maine shipowners had dealings. The ship was 227 feet (69 m) long, 42 feet (13 m) wide, by 28 feet (8.5 m) feet deep, and featured a registered displacement of 1,955 tons. Her first master was Captain Edward Brown Watts, the son of Samuel's brother and business partner Alfred Watts. The ship then went on a round-the-world voyage which lasted two years. She then operated on a route between New York, San Francisco, and the United Kingdom.  
She was described as a slow ship but good at carrying cargo. Her five outward passages were relatively slow due to poor weather, with the fastest lasting 137 days. During one voyage, she lost a rudder and anchored at Port Stanley for three months. On the eastbound voyage between San Francisco and Europe, she was faster and averaged 127 days, with the fastest lasting 110 days. On the last such voyage, she sprung a leak and stopped at St. Thomas so it could be patched by a diver.  
In 1883, the ship's namesake fled to Canada before a warrant for his arrest could be issued after he faced a warrant for embezzling and defrauding the Pacific National Bank. Samuel Watts felt betrayed by Benyon, and as a result, renamed the ship Alfred Watts to honor his brother.  
In February 1884, the ship was sold to Snow & Burgess of New York and made two round voyages; the second was between New York, Hong Kong, San Francisco, Le Havre, and Philadelphia. On 8 October 1887, the Alfred Watts left Philadelphia under command of Captain Frank Johnson, with approximately 71,000 cases of kerosene onboard bound for Hiogo. The next day, she ran aground on the Bulkhead Shoal but was undamaged and soon freed. Once underway, she left the Delaware Bay on 11 October. She was drawing 26 feet (7.9 m), her cargo was well stowed and she had sufficient ballast to keep her in trim. Seven days later, she encountered a hurricane, and heeled so heavily that vertical parts of her hull made contact with the water before the ship capsized. The ship was still afloat, but the motion knocked off the main hatch. Everyone onboard—26 crewmembers and a passenger—attempted to cover the hatch with tarpaulins before they were washed overboard. Only four seamen and the passenger avoided drowning as they reached a portion of the deck house that drifted nearby. The next day, the passenger was washed off and drowned. Two sailors attempted to swim to the ship but were not seen again. Due to the presence of blood, it was assumed they were attacked by sharks. The two survivors—Harry Magnus and Oscar Linn—came along the ship after using broken spars as paddles 36 hours after they were washed off. In another account, Captain Johnson was a sixth man who reached the deckhouse, but was also washed away.
The two survivors then managed to successfully reach the ship where only the masts were exposed. The men perched themselves on the forward-most topgallant where they lived off of tanks of fresh water and salt pork, codfish, and other foods that floated out of the holds. They remained until 20 November, when they were sighted by the bark Lizzie Perry, headed for the West Indies. A boat from the bark rescued the two men. Lizzie Perry was wrecked off Barbados on 10 December, but everyone onboard was evacuated and survived. The men from Alfred Watts returned to Philadelphia in February 1888.

 This article incorporates text from American Merchant Ships 1850-1900, by Frederick C. Matthews, a  publication from 1930, now in the public domain in the United States.

Zhongguancun Academy (ZGCA; Chinese: 北京中关村学院), also sometimes called Beijing Zhongguancun Academy (BZA), is a research-oriented higher education institution specializing in artificial intelligence (AI) and interdisciplinary research in Haidian District, Beijing, China. Established in September 2024 through a joint initiative of the Chinese Ministry of Education and the Beijing municipal government, the Academy focuses on doctoral education, scientific research, and collaboration between academia and industry.
The Academy operates a research-based educational model in which doctoral students from participating universities undertake interdisciplinary research projects under joint academic supervision. It collaborates with 31 Chinese universities and works closely with the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence, a research institution established alongside the Academy. The two institutions share academic and research resources while maintaining complementary educational and research functions.


History
Preparations for Zhongguancun Academy began on 7 June 2024. The Academy held its first opening ceremony on 13 September 2024, following an approximately three-month period of institutional preparation and student recruitment.
The Academy was established jointly by China's Ministry of Education and the Beijing municipal government. During the same period, the Beijing Municipal Science and Technology Commission established the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence as a complementary research institution.
Tie-Yan Liu, a computer scientist and former vice president of Microsoft Research Asia, became the Academy's founding president. In an April 2026 interview with CCTV News, Liu discussed the institution's establishment and its emphasis on training researchers to identify and address new scientific and technological problems.
The Academy and the Institute developed a shared institutional model intended to connect education, scientific research and technology transfer. The Academy concentrates on graduate education and academic training, while the Institute focuses on research and industrial applications.


Education


Doctoral training
Zhongguancun Academy works with 31 participating universities included in China's Double First-Class Construction initiative. Doctoral students are selected from these partner universities and undertake research and educational activities through the Academy while retaining their academic affiliation with their home universities.
The selection process includes separate tracks for students working on core AI research, interdisciplinary AI applications, and applicants whose backgrounds or research approaches differ from conventional AI training pathways. According to a June 2025 report by The Beijing News, the Academy planned to select approximately 330 doctoral students annually from participating universities.
Students are jointly supervised by academic advisers from their home institutions and researchers associated with Zhongguancun Academy. Research projects form a central component of the training model, with students able to participate in existing projects or propose new research directions.
As of June 2026, the Academy reported having trained or enrolled more than 600 doctoral students and developed more than 30 specialized courses.


Educational model
The Academy uses a project-based approach in which research activities guide students' selection of courses and other educational resources. Its curriculum includes foundational AI subjects, emerging research topics, and practical training associated with scientific or industrial problems.
In a September 2026 feature, People's Daily described how students from different academic backgrounds worked together on projects involving AI-assisted drug discovery, medical robotics and computational social science. The report also described a model in which students could independently initiate projects and recruit academic collaborators.
The Academy's curriculum includes short courses on emerging AI research topics. A 2025 report described, for example, eight-hour courses intended to be updated frequently as research developments occur.


Academic divisions
The Academy organizes its academic and research activities into three principal divisions:

AI Core (人工智能核心), covering AI theory and algorithms, foundation models, embodied intelligence, AI systems, and AI safety.
AI for Science and Technology (智能科学技术), covering applications of AI in life sciences, physical sciences, scientific computing, energy, quantum science, neuroscience, and aerospace.
AI for Humanities and Social Sciences (AI+人文社会), covering AI applications in education, finance, governance, and related fields.


Research
Research at Zhongguancun Academy focuses mostly on machine learning, AI systems, embodied intelligence, AI for scientific discovery, and applications in interdisciplinary fields. Much of this research is carried out jointly with the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence and other academic and industrial partners.


Artificial intelligence and scientific discovery
In March 2026, Zhongguancun Academy and the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence jointly announced five research projects at the Zhongguancun Forum. The projects included PhysBrain 1.0, an embodied intelligence model; RLinf-USER, a framework for online reinforcement learning; work on computational quantum chemistry; AutoSOTA, an automated research experimentation system; and a large-language-model-based social simulation system.


Interdisciplinary projects
The Academy encourages research projects involving students from multiple disciplines and institutions. Reported projects include AI-assisted protein design, medical imaging and robotic surgery, and autonomous systems for materials research.
A September 2026 People's Daily report described a student-led breast cancer minimally invasive surgery project involving doctoral students from Nankai University, Tsinghua University, and other participating universities. The project combined medical imaging, artificial intelligence and robotic control.
The same report discussed MatOS, an AI-based system for materials research developed through collaboration with industry partners.


Industry collaboration and entrepreneurship
Zhongguancun Academy works with the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence and external organizations to support applied research, technology transfer, and entrepreneurship.
As of June 2026, the two institutions had established 19 collaborative research organizations with industry partners and reported more than 120 organized research projects. According to The Beijing News, companies incubated through their combined programs had collectively raised more than 800 million yuan in financing, with more than 20 projects undergoing incubation.
Reported areas of technology transfer include embodied intelligence, AI-assisted software engineering, computational biology, and applications of AI to energy systems.


Conferences and academic activities
As a research organization, Zhongguancun Academy also participates in the organization of academic conferences and research forums on artificial intelligence.
The Academy and the Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence co-organized the inaugural International Conference on AI Scientists (ICAIS) in Beijing in November 2025, together with Tsinghua University and Westlake University.
The second conference took place from 17 to 19 October 2026 in Beijing's Haidian District and included academic presentations and discussions of AI-assisted scientific research and the future development of artificial intelligence.

The Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence (ZGCI; Chinese: 中关村人工智能研究院) is an artificial intelligence (AI) research institute headquartered in Haidian District, Beijing, China. Established in October 2024 by the Beijing Municipal Science and Technology Commission, the institute conducts research in machine learning, AI systems, embodied intelligence, scientific computing, and interdisciplinary applications of artificial intelligence.
The institute operates in coordination with Zhongguancun Academy, a research-oriented higher education institution specializing in artificial intelligence. The two institutions share academic and research resources while maintaining complementary functions: the Academy focuses primarily on education and doctoral training, while the Institute focuses on scientific research, technological development, and possible commercialization.


History
The Zhongguancun Institute of Artificial Intelligence was established on 28 October 2024, following an initiative led by the Beijing Municipal Science and Technology Commission. Its formal inauguration took place on 29 November 2024.
The institute was established alongside Zhongguancun Academy as part of an initiative to combine research, higher education, and industrial applications of artificial intelligence. The two institutions adopted a coordinated organizational model, sharing faculty and research resources while pursuing different primary responsibilities.
Tie-Yan Liu, a computer scientist and former vice president of Microsoft Research Asia, became chairman of the institute, while Shao Bin, formerly a senior principal researcher at Microsoft Research, became its president.
According to Beijing's public resources trading registry, the institute was registered as a "social organization" (社会组织), with Shao Bin listed as its legal representative.


Organization
The institute's activities encompass basic research, applied research, technology development and collaboration with industrial partners. Its research programs are coordinated with Zhongguancun Academy, with joint academic appointments and shared research infrastructure.
The institute is headed by president Shao Bin and chaired by Tie-Yan Liu, who also serves as president of Zhongguancun Academy.


Research
The institute organizes its research around three broad areas: core artificial intelligence, AI applications in the natural sciences, and AI applications in the humanities and social sciences.


Core artificial intelligence
Research in core artificial intelligence includes model architectures, optimization algorithms, reinforcement learning, AI safety, embodied intelligence, and AI infrastructure.
In March 2026, the institute and Zhongguancun Academy jointly publicly released several research project artifacts at the Zhongguancun Forum, including PhysBrain 1.0, a foundation model for embodied intelligence; RLinf-USER, a framework for online reinforcement learning; and AutoSOTA, a system for automating scientific experiments.


Artificial intelligence for natural sciences
The institute's natural-science research covers applications of machine learning and AI-based computational methods in physical sciences, life sciences, medicine, and scientific computing.
Research announced jointly with Zhongguancun Academy has included computational quantum chemistry, protein and genomic research, and applications of AI in drug discovery.


Artificial intelligence for social sciences
The institute's research also includes applications of AI in education, finance, governance, and AI ethics.


Collaboration and technology transfer
The institute works with universities, research laboratories, and companies on interdisciplinary research and commercialization opportunities.


Academic and research partnerships
In July 2025, the institute and Zhongguancun Academy established a joint AI and dance research center with the Beijing Dance Academy. The collaboration covers research in dance and artificial intelligence, including data resources, interdisciplinary research and potential applications in cultural and performing arts.
During 2025, the institute and the Academy also entered into collaborative arrangements involving the Shenzhen Medical Academy of Research and Translation and Shenzhen Bay Laboratory, covering applications of AI in biomedical research. Additional joint activities included research collaborations in AI infrastructure, finance and sports science.
In November 2025, the institute and Zhongguancun Academy signed a strategic cooperation agreement with the China University of Political Science and Law. The agreement covered interdisciplinary education, scientific research, AI governance and applications of artificial intelligence in legal studies.


Commercialization and entrepreneurship
The institute also participates in research commercialization and startup incubation. In June 2026, The Beijing News reported their incubation programs had more than 20 ongoing projects, including 11 student-led projects.
The same report stated that companies incubated through the two sister institutions (ZGCI and ZGCA) had collectively raised more than 800 million yuan in financing and had a combined pre-investment valuation exceeding 6 billion yuan. Reported areas of commercialization included embodied intelligence, AI-assisted software engineering, genomic research, and energy-system applications.

Fabryka Maszyn i Urządzeń Przemysłowych Sp. z o.o. (Industrial Machinery and Equipment Factory "FAMAD") is a Polish company based in Paczków, focused on production of equipment used in production processes related to gluing, processing, and finishing of wood; a manufacturer of machines and equipment for the furniture and wood industries, technological transport, and hydraulic cylinders.


History


Industry establishment – Kieslich Brothers (1868–1945)
In 1868, a 23 year old master blacksmith Joseph Kieslich established a workshop in Paczków where he repaired and built agricultural machinery. The small workshop developed successfully. At that time, its owner, together with his brother Johann, became interested in its further development. On a 12-morgen plot between Młynówka and Kamienica Stream, new facilities were built, a steam engine was purchased, as well as wood and metal processing equipment, resulting in a factory producing horse-drawn carriages and agricultural machinery. From 1882 onwards, in addition to agricultural machines, fire pumps were also produced. The establishment advertised itself as: "Kieslich Brothers – machinery, carriages, and fire pumps".
The company quickly gained customers' recognition and soon employed 100 workers. In the initial phase, fire pumps were built at the plant according to its own design. The two-wheeled equipment was an interesting technical solution in which, with the help of a threaded spindle, the pump was moved towards the drawbar to ensure the pump's stability during operation. This solution was registered with the Patent Office. Around 1891, the Kieslich brothers began producing fire pumps in close cooperation with the well-known company F. C. Flader. The production utilized complete pumping devices from this company and incorporated them into the wagons produced in-house. At that time, eight types of fire pumps and water transport vehicles were offered, which were showcased in various catalogs. Distinguishing the equipment produced in Paczków back then, without a factory label, from the products of the Flader company was practically impossible. In this way, a large assortment of equipment by the Kieslich brothers' company was created; however, the production of the equipment still largely involved manual work, which made the rationalization of production impossible.

After the expansion in 1902, mass production in separate halls became possible. The production of other firefighting equipment also developed. Hand-operated pressure fire pumps, motor pumps, folding ladders, turntable ladders, and steam fire engines were also manufactured, as well as equipment for transporting people and water. Upon the customer's request, complete firefighting equipment was supplied, although not all the equipment shown in the catalogs was produced at the factory. After the expiration of the Flader company's patent, the pumps were equipped with manufacturers' own pumps. The company achieved great success primarily in the production of ladders of its own design. Some of these were patented, and the factory gained wide markets. Further expansion took place in 1904 and 1908. In the meantime, the factory expanded to include buildings at Pocztowa 3 Street and on Stanisława Staszica Street. In the beginning of 1911, the company was taken over by the founder's son, certified engineer Karol Kieslich, who continued to run it under the existing name.

The area of the plant increased to 40,000 m2 (430,000 ft2) and consisted of a complex of 15 different buildings. Various types of equipment were operated by a 400 horsepower (300 kW) steam engine. The plant had its own sawmill and timber yard, which provided raw material of suitable quality for production. "Schlesische Feuerwhergerate und Maschinen-fabrik" (Silesian Fire Equipment and Machinery Factory) was the largest factory of its kind in Silesia and employed over 300 workers and laborers. In 1912, preparations began in Paczków for the production of fire engines and ladders mounted on Daimler vehicles. In the summer of 1920, the plant simultaneously produced 13 motorized fire engines, 5 motorized ladders, numerous vehicle trailers for transporting equipment, as well as a large number of hand-operated fire pumps and portable extension ladders. At the same time, orders amounted to a further 60 units of motorized fire engines and ladders.
In 1927, the factory produced horse-drawn fire pumps with manual and gasoline drives, mounted turntable ladders, and firefighting fittings. It continued to manufacture agricultural machinery: threshers of the "stift" type with manual, treadwheel, and motor drives, cleaning threshers of the "stift" and "flail" systems, seeders for mountainous and flat areas, winnowers and grain cleaning mills, and three-roll mangles for domestic use. Thanks to the export of equipment, the plant survived the severe period of hyperinflation between 1922 and 1923 and returned to its previous condition. However, the global economic crisis in 1929 caused a lack of orders and ultimately led to the company's bankruptcy. With the collapse of the enterprise, the last owner, Karol Kieslich, and 350 employees lost their source of income. After the company's failure, Karol Kieslich ran a small business: "Certified Engineer Karol Kieslich – Equipment Plant for Firefighting Equipment and Accessories", selling equipment produced by other companies.
The last mention of its existence dates back to September 1938. The main building complex between Młynówka and Kamienica Stream was purchased by the town, which set up a labor camp there. In 1938, the factory buildings along with the land were resold to the Müller brothers, who established chemical industry production there. The premises on Staszica Street were purchased in 1929 by the Biedermann brothers, who set up a factory for gilded and natural frames. In the same year, one of the brothers also bought part of the plant at Pocztowa 8 Street, where artificial wines and beer malt were produced.


Herbert Hauschild – Firefighting Equipment Factory
In 1929, the former paint shop at Pocztowa 3 Street was taken over by Hauschild Junior. On its basis, he established a company under the name: "Herbert Hauschild Feuerwehr-geratefabrik" (Herbert Hauschild Firefighting Equipment Factory). He employed some of the specialists from the firefighting equipment factory, successfully producing fixed extendable ladders and ladders on vehicle chassis, as well as sports equipment: skis, sleds, vaulting horses, ladders, and gym rails. During World War II, the plant was considered important for fire defense and, as such, was primarily focused on the production of fire ladders, including 12 m (39 ft 4 in) two-wheeled mechanical ladders. Ammunition crates were also produced. At that time, the plant employed 45 workers in two shifts.


Match factory
The match factory (Zündholtzfabrik), Gierth & Ergmann, was established in Paczków at the current address of Kopernika 1 Street in 1842. Phosphorus-sulfur matches were produced manually. The factory was acquired in 1877 by Julius Huch. It received the name: "Fabrik chemischer Zündwaren" (Factory of Chemical Ignition Materials). Huch introduced far-reaching technical innovations, modernized the plant, and over the years, the existing phosphorus-sulfur matches were replaced with safety matches. The plant faced various adversities. Two fires in Paczków destroyed most of the facility. The introduction of a match tax, competition with large corporations, as well as the dumping sales practiced by the Swedes and Russians, often led to difficult periods for the factory. When Julius Huch moved to Berlin, he left the management of the plant to his long-time collaborator, Director Albert Huch.


Fabryka Części Pożarniczych i Sportowych (1945–1951)
After the end of World War II, as part of the industrial nationalization reform, the plant was nationalized on 1 July 1945, and began operating under the name: "Firefighting and Sports Parts Factory" (Polish: Fabryka Części Pożarniczych i Sportowych) Until April 1946, the plant was subordinated to the management of the Automotive Industry in Warsaw. The company had an adequate machinery park and a six-month supply of raw materials. Work in the plant was carried out in three rooms. The workforce consisted of 16 people, including 2 white-collar employees. The position of manager, and from 1950, director of the plant, was assumed by Marian Kostka. In 1945, the plant produced a 12 m (39 ft 4 in) two-wheeled mechanical ladder (1 unit), Szczerbowski ladders (10 units), hook ladders (10 units), and extension ladders (40 units).
The plant encountered difficulties in streamlining production, so the first products were manufactured according to German standards. In addition, small quantities of sleds and skis of various lengths were produced. Between 1946 and 1947, the plant was under the Southern Association of the Metal Industry in Głuchołazy, while from 1947 it was subordinated to the Local Industry Board based in Katowice. Between 1949 and 1950, the company took over the mechanical carpentry workshop at Pocztowa 8 Street, as well as the facilities of the former match factory at the current Kopernika 1 Street. There, after adapting the premises, the production department for ladders and wooden semi-finished products was located, while the metal production department remained at its former location at Pocztowa 3 Street. During this period, the plant included:

Locksmith at Pocztowa 3 Street
Assembly department at Kopernika 1 Street
Mechanical carpentry at Pocztowa 8 street
Graphic works at Wojska Polskiego 28 Street
A larger production area, with 128 employees at the end of 1951, contributed to the development of the company, as evidenced by the expanded range of products and their improved quality. The main specialty of the plant was fire ladders, manufactured in accordance with the standards and requirements of the Main Fire Brigade Headquarters. The plant in Paczków became the only producer of fire ladders in Poland. At that time, the plant produced:

Two-wheeled 12 and 17 m (39 ft 4 in and 55 ft 9 in) ladders
14 m (45 ft 11 in) rod ladders
10 m (32 ft 10 in) Szczerbowski ladders
Hook ladders
Column ladders
Socket ladders
Heavy-duty 5 and 6 m (16 ft 5 in and 19 ft 8 in) portable ladders
Light 3.8 m (12 ft 6 in) leaning ladders

The 1949 VFA Grand Final was an Australian rules football match contested between the Williamstown Football Club and the Oakleigh Football Club at the St Kilda Cricket Ground on 1 October 1949. It was the 41st grand final of the Victorian Football Association (VFA), held to determine the premiers of the 1949 VFA season.
In a close-fought match, Oakleigh led the match at the first three breaks, before Williamstown were able to take the lead in a see-sawing final quarter. Oakleigh led by three points with only seconds remaining, but Johnny Walker was able to kick a running goal from the boundary line to give Williamstown a three-point lead moments before the final bell rang. It was Williamstown's fifth VFA premiership and Oakleigh's first grand final loss, although they won the premiership the following year.
For many years, it was thought that Williamstown's Ron Todd – who was playing his final VFA match – had missed his chance to kick his 1000th goal in senior football (including his career at Collingwood in the Victorian Football League and Williamstown combined) after he passed the ball to Allan Strang, who then passed it to Walker to kick the winning goal in the final seconds of the match. However, official records were amended in 2017 to credit Todd with 1,001 career goals instead of 999, meaning he had in fact kicked his 1000th goal during the grand final.
The crowd of 40,000 people at the St Kilda Cricket Ground was the second-highest attendance for a VFA/VFL grand final (equal with 1908 and 1953; only behind the 1939 grand final), which brought in a record £3,388 in ticket sales.


Match summary

In a pre-match ceremony at the ground, Preston captain-coach Jack Blackman was presented with the J. J. Liston Trophy as the competition's best-and-fairest player by VFA president Frank Hartnett, with immigration minister Arthur Calwell in attendance.


First quarter
Williamstown had the wind advantage in the first quarter, but Oakleigh's Alan Scott kicked the first goal of the match, before Williamstown equalled through a goal from John Molyneux. Oakleigh's second goal was kicked by captain-coach George Smeaton after a pass from Max Wenn. Although Oakleigh had the ball in their forward line for much of the first quarter, they struggled to convert against Williamstown defender Jack Danckert. Much of the crowd was focused on Ron Todd, who was thought to be sitting at 994 senior goals going into the match (in reality, he was at 996 goals); he kicked two behinds during the first quarter.


Second quarter
Todd kicked the first goal of the second quarter after he was awarded a free kick in Williamstown's forward line. Oakleigh had their first chance of the term to kick after a free was awarded to Wenn, but his kick fell short. Another chance for Todd was prevented after Alex Boyle knocked the ball out of play, with Clive Watson evading defenders to kick Oakleigh's first goal of the second quarter. While the defensive efforts of Danckert were still strong, Oakleigh managed another two goals – one from Jim Edwards and another from Watson – to lead by 25 points at half-time, having had the ball in their forward line for the vast majority of the quarter.


Third quarter
Oakleigh opened the third quarter with a goal from Ray Finn, giving them a match-high 31-point lead. Williamstown's Lou Salvas marked the ball in the centre of the ground and kicked it to Todd for a mark; Todd's shot for goal went wide. However, he had another opportunity when he marked over the pack and kicked his second goal of the day from an awkward angle. Williamstown half-back Bill Wells slung Smeaton to the ground but the incident was missed by the umpire, giving Williamstown another chance for a goal through a kick to Allan Strang, although he missed and the ball quickly returned to the centre; Norman Bernard snapped a goal over his shoulder shortly after to reduce the margin to 21 points. Todd kicked his third goal of the match after marking in front, then kicked a fourth after a pass from Johnny Walker from 50 metres out; this was, in reality, his 1000th senior goal, but it was believed at the time to have been his 998th. Walker also kicked his first goal late in the quarter to reduce the margin to just three points at the final break.


Final quarter

Wenn opened the scoring for Oakleigh early in the final quarter, while Williamstown were kicking into the wind. Todd missed another goal but was soon awarded a free on the half-forward line and kicked his fifth goal of the day; this was incorrectly thought to have been his 999th senior goal. After several attacking plays from both teams, Keith Abberton broke through the pack to kick his first goal of the match and give Williamstown the advantage and its first lead of the match. Much of the play became concentrated in Oakleigh's forward half; Bernie Perkins took a mark in the pocket to kick the ball to Smeaton, who marked and kicked truly to give Oakleigh a two-point lead.
Williamstown had another chance when Todd marked the ball and passed it to Lou Barker, but his kick was smothered and the ball quickly returned to Oakleigh's half. With around one minute remaining in the match, Oakleigh led by three points (8.14 to 9.5) and the ball entered their forward line, but Williamstown was able to clear it and attack. At this point in the match, players from both teams were visibly tired and were misjudging marking attempts, but Williamstown were able to get control of the football. Todd (who had an opportunity to kick his sixth goal of the match) passed the ball to Strang, who kicked to Walker in the forward pocket; Walker played on and kicked a goal to give Williamstown the lead. The final bell sounded moments after play resumed, with Williamstown winning the grand final by three points.


Scorecard


Teams
Teams for the grand final were announced on 30 September 1949.

Herengracht 458, also known as Huize Goudstikker, is a double house on the Herengracht in Amsterdam, in the Golden Bend (Gouden Bocht) between the Leidsestraat and the Spiegelstraat. The building has national monument (rijksmonument) status.
The house was built in the 17th century for the merchant Martinus Alewijn and was extensively remodelled in 1875 by the architect Willem Hamer. Jacques Goudstikker had his art dealership here from 1926 until he fled the Netherlands in May 1940.


Description


Exterior
The front facade dates from 1875 and was built in a traditional manner. It is a brick facade with a straight cornice, sandstone corner pilasters and a sandstone central projection topped by a curved pediment. The attic carries two vases, and festoons decorate the facade at the level where the bel étage meets the first floor.


Interior
The great hall at the back was probably fitted out by Dirck Wuytiers van de Werve. Its decoration included a ceiling painting by Gerard de Lairesse in three panels showing the fall of Phaethon, and late 17th-century tapestries with scenes from Greek history. In 1930 the De Lairesse ceiling was still in this room. Ceiling paintings by Jacob de Wit from about 1720, part of Wuytiers's interior, later ended up in a country house in Hoevelaken; the auction house F. Muller sold parts of the interior in 1903 and 1928.
The 1875 remodelling added a staircase rising through the full height of the house, lit by a lantern in the roof. It received carved walnut banisters, and three Flemish tapestries were hung on its upper walls. Behind the staircase a dining room was built in an extension into the garden, panelled in walnut in the Italian Renaissance style and lined with gilt leather.
After 1926 Goudstikker had fittings from other canal houses installed. From Herengracht 609 came overdoor paintings by Jacob de Wit and a chimneypiece carved by B. and T. de Wilde showing Venus in the forge of Vulcan, which the burgomaster Gerrit Hooft had installed there in 1743. A mantelpiece from the last quarter of the 18th century, from Herengracht 452, was placed in a back room. Under Goudstikker the house also held gilt leather from the convent of Catharinendal in South Brabant, a marble fountain in the Louis XVI style in the corridor, and, in an upstairs room, four wall paintings of mountain landscapes along the Rhine signed by H.W. Schweickhard. Three period rooms on the first floor, each panelled in a different style, served as showrooms.


History


17th and 18th centuries
When the building plots were auctioned in 1665, Martinus Alewijn bought plots 19 and 20, each 26 voet (Amsterdam feet) wide and 180 voet deep, for a total of ƒ14,654. Alewijn traded in partnership with Adriaan Lampsens. In 1668 he also acquired plots 61 and 62 on the Keizersgracht, which allowed him to build a stable behind his garden with an exit onto the Keizersgracht. Alewijn had the house built on plots 19 and 20; according to Amsterdam Monumentenstad it was completed in 1670.
Alewijn died in 1684. Dirck Wuytiers van de Werve first rented the house from Alewijn's children and bought it on 17 April 1696, paying ƒ52,000, plus ƒ2,000 for the gilt leather, the chimneypieces and the standing and lying plates in the house. Wuytiers had no children. When his estate was divided in 1734, the house went to his niece Maria Elisabeth de Wale van Ankeveen, who sold it on 18 May 1738 for ƒ100,000 to Nicolaas Pancras. The sale included almost all the wall hangings, the paintings in the ceilings, on the chimneypieces and above the doors, and the fixed mirrors.
Pancras died in 1739. Through a legacy from his widow, Anna Elisabeth Geelvinck, the house passed in 1758 to Anna Jacoba Geelvinck, a niece of the widow and the wife of Abraham Dedel. Dedel, who was among other things burgomaster in 1788, died in 1798 and left the house to Johanna Albertina Geelvinck, the wife of Albert Cornelis Schuyt.


19th century

The Schuyts sold the house in 1801 for ƒ44,000 to Marie Aletta Koenen, the widow of J.W. Winter. In 1821 it passed for ƒ40,000 to Marcus Broen. On 1 November 1862 Jacobus Hermanus Insinger bought the whole property for ƒ51,500, and after his death in 1874 it passed to his son Maurits Herman Insinger.
In 1875 Maurits Insinger had the house remodelled by Willem Hamer. The high front steps were removed, the entrance was moved to street level and the vestibule became a small boudoir. In 1930 J.F.L. de Balbian Verster judged that this remodelling did not accord with the ideas on repair and restoration of his own day. Insinger died in 1891; his widow lived in the house until her death in 1902.


20th century

At an auction on 29 April 1903 the art collector Pieter Langerhuizen Lambertszoon, of the Crailoo estate near Laren, bought the property for ƒ119,000. He had the architect Herman Walenkamp restore the house in line with its original layout. The high front steps were rebuilt and the garden was laid out again. Hamer's staircase was kept. Langerhuizen housed much of his collection there but never lived in the house. After his death in 1918 his executors sold it for ƒ260,000 to the Nederlandsche Handel-Maatschappij, which set up offices there and, under an exemption, built an office building in the garden.
In June 1926 the art dealership J. Goudstikker bought the house for ƒ239,000; until then the firm had been based in the Kalverstraat. The wooden building in the garden had to be demolished, after which the garden was laid out again under the supervision of the garden architect J.R. Koning. In May 1940 Jacques Goudstikker left the house with his family for IJmuiden. He fled the Netherlands by ship and died in an accident on the way.

On 13 July 1940 the firm sold the house, together with Nijenrode Castle, the country house Oostermeer in Ouderkerk aan de Amstel, the trade name and part of the paintings, to Alois Miedl for ƒ550,000. After the war the property was registered in the firm's name again in 1949 as part of the restoration of rights, and the settlement of the Miedl transaction was completed in 1952.
On 27 March 1947 the British Council opened an office in the house. In 1952 it was announced that the Bureau voor Muziek-Auteursrecht would occupy the house, which until shortly before had housed the British Council; the architect A.J. Kleykamp was commissioned to convert the building.


Monument
Herengracht 458 was entered in the national monuments register on 21 May 1970 under number 1857. The register description dates the double house to the 18th century or earlier.

Crimdon Dene Lifeboat Station was located at Crimdon Beach at Crimdon, approximately 4 miles (6.4 km) north of Hartlepool, on the east coast of England in County Durham.
An Inshore lifeboat station was established here in 1966, by the Royal National Lifeboat Institution (RNLI).
After operating just three different Inshore lifeboats over a period of 27 years, Crimdon Dene Lifeboat Station was closed in 1993.


History
In 1962, the number of rescues or attempted rescues by All-weather lifeboats in the summer months was 98, with the number of lives rescued being 133. In 1963, in response to an increasing amount of water-based leisure activity, the RNLI began trials of small fast Inshore lifeboats, placed at various locations around the country. These were easily launched with just a few people, ideal to respond quickly to local emergencies. This quickly proved to be very successful. In 1963, there were 226 rescues or attempted rescues in the summer months, as a result of which 225 lives were saved.
In 1966, it was decided to establish an Inshore lifeboat station on the beach at Crimdon Dene. The Inshore lifeboat D-106, originally intended for Dún Laoghaire, was assigned to the station. The new boat could provide a much faster response for incidents in the area, than the 46-foot Watson-class All-weather lifeboat The Princess Royal (Civil Service No.7) (ON 828), stationed 4 miles (6.4 km) to the south at Hartlepool. Initially, Inshore lifeboats were placed on station just for the summer season.
The lifeboat was stored underneath the beach toilet block, a former World War II lookout post.
After nine years service, the D-class (RFD PB16) inshore lifeboat was replaced in 1975 by a D-class (Zodiac III) lifeboat (D-237), funded by the Ancient Order of Foresters. On Saturday 10 April 1976, approximately 60 members of the Order attended the station at Crimdon Dene, along with their families, for the formal handing over, dedication and blessing of the new lifeboat.
Crimdon Dene would get their third and final lifeboat in 1987. The new 16 ft (4.9 m) lifeboat this time was a D-class (EA16), and was one of two provided from the legacy of Mrs Dorothy Martin of Bexhill-on-Sea, with the other being placed at Torbay. At a ceremony at Crindon Centre Club on 12 August 1987, the lifeboat was accepted to the care of Crimdon Dene lifeboat station by George Lennard, Honorary Secretary. After a service of dedication by Rev. Charles Cole, Vicar of St Andrew's Church, Blackhall, branch chairman David O'Donnell invited Mrs Linda Barker, wife of councillor A. Barker, chairman of Easington District Council, to name the lifeboat Norman Victor Hickling (D-337), after the late brother of Mrs Martin, who had been killed in action during World War II.
In an ideal world, a lifeboat should never be needed. However, under-used assets are better placed elsewhere, and this would be the fate of Crimdon Dene Lifeboat Station. In the last eight years of operation, the lifeboat was called just eight times, with no lives saved. Crimdon Dene Lifeboat Station was closed at the end of the summer season, on 31 October 1993. The lifeboat on station, Norman Victor Hickling (D-337), was transferred to the Relief fleet.
Following a period of vandalism, the former station building was finally demolished in 1999.


Station honours
The following are awards made at Crimdon Dene.

Resuscitation Certificate, awarded by the Royal Humane Society
A. N. Phillips – 1967
I. A. Goodrich – 1967

The Slovene Ethnographic Museum (Slovenski etnografski muzej) is a museum and ethnographic institution in Ljubljana, Slovenia. Its primary focus is the documentation and presentation of Slovene culture and heritage. However, it also features exhibitions focusing on non-European cultures, with approximately a quarter of its material collection comprising items from these regions.
The museum also contains a library and a documentation department open to visitors. Additionally, it has facilities to host events such as lectures and conferences.  


History

The origins of the museum can be traced to the ethnographic collections of the Provincial Museum of Carniola (now the National Museum of Slovenia), established in 1821. However, the museum recognises its founding date to be in 1923, when it became independent of the National Museum to be established as the Royal Ethnographic Museum, formerly the Institute of Ethnography within its parent institution. Slovene ethnologist Niko Županič was appointed as the first director of the museum. In 1941, it was renamed as the Ethnographic Museum, and in 1964 as the Slovene Ethnographic Museum. Collections were displayed at several castles surrounding Ljubljana. 
Located in the Rudolfinum building with the National Museum until 1997, it then moved into its present address, in a former Austro-Hungarian barracks from the late-19th century, which was constructed for the Habsburg army infantry. Due to limited floor space, the Goričane Castle had housed the non-European collections until 2001. Construction of a new exhibition building was completed in 2004 and the museum was expanded.  


Collections
The museum holds roughly 40,000 items, with 30,000 in Slovene collections and 10,000 in non-European collections. 29,000 of these items are inventoried in the museum's documentation system. The Documentation Department of the museum also retains a digitised collection of pictorial material, approximately 110,000 in number.
The Slovene collections are categorised and maintained by curators across seven departments, some of which are the Clothing Culture and Textiles Collection, the Rural Economy and Transport Collection, and the Spiritual Culture Collection. These collections help in understanding the everyday life of Slovenians, and in understanding the similarities and differences between it and neighbouring cultures. The Slovenian collections were inherited from the museum's predecessors, and had initially been created in the late nineteenth century. 
The non-European collections of the museum are categorised by continent and donor. Several items in the collection were donated by seamen and Catholic missionaries, who would travel outside the region; other noted professions that donated items included explorers, merchants, and diplomats. Examples of such persons include Ignatius Knoblecher and Baron Anton Codelli.


Exhibitions
The museum hosts a few permanent exhibitions, and temporary exhibitions throughout the year. The temporary exhibitions are usually displayed for a period between two and six months. The first permanent exhibition, titled Between Nature and Culture, was opened in 2006, and exhibits over 3000 objects relating to everyday and festive Slovenian life. The remaining permanent exhibitions were set up between 2022 and 2025. Temporary exhibitions have focused on subjects such as the colonial history of Tanzania and Ukrainian embroidered clothing.
The museum also hosts "virtual tours" on its website, which allow the viewer to see the exhibits as though they were in the museum. This is achieved through a series of 360-degree panoramas, which may optionally be viewed with VR headsets for a more immersive experience.


Programs
The museum offers guided tours with prior booking.
The museum includes a pottery studio that offers activities involving the production of clay-based and ceramic articles. The studio offers several courses and workshops, as well as individual classes. The museum also has a weaving atelier, from which visitors may purchase products such as scarves and jackets. The atelier also hosts individual weaving courses. For children, the museum hosts educational events such as video projections and lectures.


Access and accessibility
The information in this section is accurate as of November 2026.
The museum is less than ten minutes by walk from the main Ljubljana bus and train station. City buses 2, 9, 12, 25, 27 B, and 27 NS can also be used; stopping at Friškovec on Masarykova street leaves a less-than-five-minute walk to the museum. The museum is open from 10 am to 6 pm from Tuesday to Sunday; on Thursdays, the hours are extended to 8 pm. It remains closed on Mondays and national holidays. The library and documentation sections have opening hours specified on Mondays as well, but not on Saturdays and Sundays.
Entry costs 8 euros for adults and 5 euros for children, with discounted rates for pensioners and families. Group tours can also be arranged (with a tour guide) if notice is given in advance; depending on the size of the group, rates vary, but are slightly higher per person than the previously listed individual rates. Additional charges may apply to participate in workshops or other programs hosted by the museum from time to time. Admission is free for tour guides, the unemployed, people with disabilities, museum donors, and holders of valid membership cards. It is also free on the first Sunday of every month.
The museum has facilities for the specially abled, such as magnifiers, audio guides, and tactile maps for the visually impaired, and toilets for people with disabilities, and all exhibitions are accessible to wheelchair users.
