Chandler Robbins (February 14, 1810 – September 11, 1882) was an American Unitarian minister and pastor of the Second Church, Boston, a historic congregation where he succeeded Ralph Waldo Emerson as pastor. He graduated from Harvard College and Harvard Divinity School and was active in Boston intellectual circles. 


Early life and education
Robbins was born in Lynn, Massachusetts, on February 14, 1810. His parents were Peter Gilman Robbins, a physician, and Abba Dowse, a schoolteacher. His grandfather was Chandler Robbins, D.D., minister of the First Parish Church in Plymouth, and his great-grandfather was Philemon Robbins, pastor at Branford, Connecticut. Chandler and his younger brother, Samuel Dowse Robbins, both became Unitarian pastors. 
In childhood, Robbins received private schooling, including tutelage from Edward Bliss Emerson in Roxbury. He graduated from Harvard College in 1829 and briefly attended Harvard Divinity School before dropping out to spend a year teaching at the Boston Latin School. He then returned to Harvard Divinity School, graduating in 1833. 


Career
Robbins was ordained December 4, 1833, as pastor of the Second Church, Boston, succeeding Ralph Waldo Emerson in this office. Robbins held this pastorate for 41 years, retiring in December 1874. He received the degree of Doctor of Divinity from Harvard in 1855. He served as chaplain of the Massachusetts Senate in 1834 and chaplain of the Massachusetts House of Representatives in 1836. He edited The Christian Register, the leading Unitarian weekly, from January 1837 to April 1839, concurrently serving on the executive committee of the American Unitarian Association. He also served as president of the Benevolent Fraternity of Churches from 1862 to 1872.  
Robbins was active in the Massachusetts Historical Society, serving in various society offices and producing articles and lectures on colonial-era Massachusetts and other historical topics. He became a member of the Massachusetts Historical Society in 1845, a member of the American Antiquarian Society in 1857, and a fellow of the American Academy of Arts and Sciences in 1858. 
Robbins participated in at least five meetings of the Transcendental Club, together with other liberal Unitarian ministers such as Ralph Waldo Emerson, Frederic Henry Hedge, and Theodore Parker. He hosted at least one meeting at his house in January 1839. 
Robbins researched and published A History of the Second Church in Boston (John Wilson & Son, 1852). He edited the works of Henry Ware Jr., published in four volumes in 1846–47. He compiled The Social Hymn Book (James Munroe & Company, 1843) and Hymn Book for Christian Worship (Walker, Wise, & Company, 1864). He also printed many of his sermons and lectures.


Personal life
Days after his ordination in December 1833, Robbins married Mary Eliza Frothingham (1812–1870), a woman from Boston. The couple had seven children. Mary Robbins died during the summer of 1870 after a prolonged illness. In June 1874, Robbins remarried to Sarah Ripley (Fiske) Willard (1839–1905), a widow some 30 years his junior, and had three more children with her.
In 1870, Robbins accidentally struck his head against the corner of an open door, causing progressive total blindness in both eyes. He died in Weston, Massachusetts, on September 11, 1882, at the age of 72. He was buried at Mount Auburn Cemetery. 


References

The Jesus Generation is the upcoming ninth studio album by the American contemporary Christian musician Forrest Frank. The album is scheduled for release on December 4, 2026, through River House Records. Its twenty tracks were primarily written and produced by Frank himself, containing additional production credits from Axe Williams, D. Mills, Malachi O'Connor, Jordan Padilla, Josh Holiday, and Pera Krstajić, and additional writing credits from Krstajić. It contains guest appearances from Jonathan Pokluda, KB, Lecrae, Jvke, Zvc, Michael W. Smith, Lost & Found, and Elevation Rhythm. The album is thematically a "social commentary on the country's current spiritual revival."
The Jesus Generation was supported with the release of two preceding singles. "Okay!", the album's lead single, was released on May 3, 2026, and reached number 22 on the Billboard Bubbling Under Hot 100 Singles chart and number 4 on the Hot Christian Songs chart. "Jesus Is Alive!", the album's second single, was released on May 8, 2026, and peaked at number 8 on the Bubbling Under Hot 100 Singles chart, as well as topping the Hot Christian Songs chart. The album was also promoted by three promotional singles, including "What a Life" on October 30, 2026, "Go! Go! Go!" on November 6, 2026, "My Little Miracle" on November 13, 2026, and "New Season" on November 20, 2026. "What a Life" peaked at number 10 on the Billboard Hot Christian Songs chart, "Go! Go! Go!" peaked at number 29, and "My Little Miracle" peaked at number 24.
The Jesus Generation was promoted with Frank's headling Jesus Generation Tour, which featured guest acts Tori Kelly, Cory Asbury, and the Figs, and reached 29 locations within the United States. Selling 500,000 tickets within the first half-month of announcement and later drawing an audience of 519,000 people, the tour set attendance records at several arenas and has been recognized as the most successful tour of Christian music to date. An eponymous tour documentary, created in collaboration with Lionsgate and Kingdom Story Company, is scheduled for release in 2027.


Release and promotion

The lead single from The Jesus Generation, "Okay!", was released on May 3, 2026. Frank wrote the song over livestreaming on Instagram. On June 29, 2026, the song was released to Christian radio in the United States. In late April 2026, Frank began releasing teasers for "Jesus Is Alive!" to social media platforms. Seeing the song's success, he launched a promotional campaign in which he challenged other performers — primarily independent Christian musicians — to participate in recording an original rap verse or producing a remix of song. The campaign was a success, attracting attention from both secular musicians as well as Christian musicians. On May 8, 2026, the song was released as the second single from The Jesus Generation. Its release was promoted with the release of a lyric video, which was uploaded to YouTube.
On October 28, 2026, Frank announced through social media the release of the upcoming album, and it was announced that a preceding single from the album would be released on October 30. Early teasers of the album's tracks revealed which collaborators would give guest appearances on the album, and Frank sought advice on social media as to which tracks should be released ahead of the full album. Of the nine tracks teased, those selected were "What a Life", released as a promotional single on October 30, 2026, "Go! Go! Go!", on November 6, 2026, "My Little Miracle" on November 13, 2026, and "New Season" on November 20, 2026. With the release of the "Go! Go! Go!", The Jesus Generation was made available for pre-order. A lyric video, uploaded to YouTube, was released in promotion of each of the four tracks.


Tour
In promotion of The Jesus Generation, Frank headlined the supporting Jesus Generation Tour in affiliation with TPR, which featured guest acts Tori Kelly, Cory Asbury, and The Figs. Beginning on June 1, 2026, at the BOK Center in Tulsa, Oklahoma, and lasting until August 1, at the Globe Life Field in Arlington, Texas, the tour visited 29 locations within the United States. It was first announced in December 2025; tickets and VIP packages were made available for an exclusive presale at Frank's website beginning on December 10, and on December 12 were made available to the general public. Visiting several arenas and stadiums, the tour would mark Frank's most amibitious headlining tour, and his first headlining outdoor in-the-round stadium tour. The Jesus Generation Tour was Frank's third headlining tour overall.


Reception
The Jesus Generation Tour was a commercial success. Jonathan Roberts, speaking to Billboard on behalf of the United Talent Agency, claimed that it was "the most in-demand tour in Christian music history," and both CCM Magazine and Crosswalk have commended it as the biggest Christian music tour in history, drawing an audience of over 519,000 attendees. An estimated 50,000 audience members were converted to Christianity during live performances on the tour. The tour was significantly more successful than previous headlining tours of Frank's; his Child of God Tour in 2024 and Child of God Tour Part 2 in 2025 saw a total attendance of 48,246 and 214,626, respectively.
Many tickets sold within the presale, and within ten days of its announcement the tour had already sold 470,000 tickets. The tour ultimately drew an audience of 519,000 attendees. Many of the tour's venus sold-out within days of announcement, and several venues set attendance records. Each location drew an average of over 17,000 audience members, while some saw as many as 35,000. United Talent Agency's Jeffrey Hasson attributed the tour's success to Frank's ability to "[connect] with a generation of fans" and appear "at the forefront of a cultural moment that is very special and very rare," and the tour's success has also lent itself to his attraction of various ages and demographics.


Film
In October 2026, Frank announced through Instagram that he, in partnership with Lionsgate and Kingdom Story Company, would be releasing a documentary film, containing live performances and behind-scenes-footage from the tour. Similarly titled The Jesus Generation, the film is scheduled for release to theatres in 2027. Although a director and specific release date had not yet been announced, it was announced that the film would feature appearances from Rick Rubin, Philip Anthony Mitchell, and Sadie Robertson Huff. A trailer was released in promotion of the film.


Commercial performance
"Okay!" received notable success early-on; within the ten days prior to its release, teasers for the song had accumulated 50 million views on social media platforms.  Within its first charting week, the song debuted at number 14 on the Billboard Hot Christian Songs chart and entered the Digital Song Sales chart at number 11. On October 3, 2026 it reached its peak at number 4 on the former. On September 5, the song entered the Billboard Bubbling Under Hot 100 Singles chart at number 25, and on September 19 peaked at number 22. On the Christian Airplay chart the song reached number 7. The song also received international success; in Australia it peaked at number 4 on Today's Christian Music's Australian Christian Airplay chart.
"Jesus Is Alive!" debuted at number 17 on the Billboard Hot Christian Songs chart. The following week, the song entered the Bubbling Under Hot 100 Singles chart at number 8 and the Digital Song Sales chart at number 13, and rose to top the Hot Christian Songs chart. The song's success marked Frank's second leader on the chart, following "Your Way's Better" in 2025. The sixteen-position jump led "Jesus Is Alive!" to tie alongside Reba McEntire and Lauren Daigle's "Back to God", MercyMe's "I Can Only Imagine", and Daigle's "You Say" as the biggest jump to the top position in the history of the Hot Christian Songs chart. The rise is due in majority to streaming support; the track saw a 199% increase in streams for that week.
Two of the album's promotional singles, "What a Life" and "Go! Go! Go!", both debuted at their peak positions on the Billboard Hot Christian Songs chart, with "What a Life" reaching number 10 and "Go! Go! Go!" reaching number 29.

Notes

Cameron Greenlee (also known as GreenTree Keys) is an American keyboardist, composer, and record producer based on Block Island, Rhode Island. He toured internationally for five years as the keyboardist for the Jamaican ska band The Skatalites, performing at the Montreux Jazz Festival in 2011, recording on their studio album Walk With Me (2012), and contributing tracks to Platinum Ska (2016). He is also the keyboardist for the reggae collective the Naya Rockers and serves as organizational president and artistic director of Block Island's ConserFest.


Early life
According to his official biography, Greenlee was born in the Bronx, New York, and began studying classical piano at the age of five. During his school years, he played both piano and drum set, and also studied tone-wheel and pipe organ, harpsichord, and Brazilian and Latin percussion.


Career


Early session work and Cameron & the Trenton Street Collaborative (2008–2009)
In 2008, Greenlee was credited with keyboards across ten tracks on Jamaican singer Ernie Smith's studio album Country Mile ("You Are a Lion", "Trouble Trouble", "Zion Bound", "One Man the Same Man", "Kin Kat Pupalick", "Deadly Night in Kingston", "Coming Back from the Floor", "Break Away", "Alone Again", and "Longfellow Serenade") and recorded organ on "Dem A Wondaa" and keyboards on "Reala Dub" for Infinite Dub (Dubs and Instrumentals), a collaborative dub album by the Virgin Islands roots-reggae band Midnite and Lustre Kings.
On 28 August 2009, Greenlee released his debut five-track EP as a bandleader, Cameron & the Trenton Street Collaborative, on the roots-reggae label Naya Records. The release features three original instrumentals composed by Greenlee ("Nor'Easter", "In the Wolves Den", and "Out of Darkness") alongside arrangements of Jackie Mittoo's "Lazy Bones" and "Please Sunrise" (credited on the EP sleeve to Augustus Pablo, after the song written by Barbara Acklin and Eugene Record). Co-produced by Greenlee and drummer Nathan Sabanayagam and mixed and mastered by Will Holland and Eric Welsh at Chillhouse Studios in Charlestown, Massachusetts, the sessions featured Jamaican saxophonist Cedric "Im" Brooks, guitarist Andy Bassford, trumpeter Kevin Batchelor, trombonist Andrae Murchison, bassists Dan Edinberg and M'Talewa Thomas, reed and guitar player Frankie "Ranks" Moniz, melodica player Yusaku Yoshimura, guitarist Geoff Scott, and percussionist Alex Beram.


The Skatalites

Greenlee spent five years touring internationally as the keyboardist for the Jamaican ska band The Skatalites. His documented live appearances with the group include their performance at Miles Davis Hall during the Montreux Jazz Festival on 1 July 2011, their May 2012 UK appearance in Bristol, and 2013 touring alongside founding members Lester Sterling and Doreen Shaffer.
Greenlee served as the keyboardist on the band's studio album Walk With Me (2012), which featured the final studio recordings of founding drummer Lloyd Knibb. Basic tracks for the album were recorded by Bill Sample at Don One Sound Studio in Brooklyn, New York, with Greenlee's Yamaha piano and Hammond B3 organ overdubs recorded by Will Holland at Chillhouse Studios in Charlestown, Massachusetts; Greenlee was also credited on the 2012 Wrasse Records CD sleeve as editor of the liner notes and, alongside his bandmates, as an executive producer. On the Liquidator Music edition on Qobuz and on Discogs, Greenlee is credited as the composer of Track 7, "Piece for Peace" (and as producer on Discogs, noting a printing error on the initial 2012 Wrasse CD sleeve that had repeated Andrae Murchison's credits from Track 6). On the band's 2016 album Platinum Ska (Island Empire Records), Greenlee is credited with keyboards on "Calabash" and "Bamako Ska" and vocals on "Calabash", "Bamako Ska", "Dub Calabash", and "Bamadub Ska".


The Naya Rockers and GreenTree Music
As a member of the Naya Rockers, the Boston-based live and studio collective associated with Naya Records founded by drummer and producer Nathan Sabanayagam, Greenlee has performed and recorded alongside Sabanayagam and bassist Nate Edgar, and in his career has backed veteran Jamaican artists including Ernie Smith, Clinton Fearon, and Sister Nancy, as well as modern roots-reggae artists including Bushman and Iba Mahr.
In 2024 and 2025, Greenlee recorded keyboards for the Naya Rockers' studio album Higher Education (2025), a collaborative benefit project supporting the Alpha School of Music in Kingston, Jamaica. Basic rhythm tracks for the album were recorded with engineer Wade Koeman at Floki Studios in Iceland alongside Sabanayagam, bassist Nate Edgar, and guitarist Andy Bassford, with additional overdubs by guitarist Earl "Chinna" Smith in Kingston and mixing by Stephen Stewart, Michael "Boxy" Howell, and Sabanayagam at Tuff Gong Studios. Ahead of the album, Greenlee was featured on the 2024 singles "Higher Education" (with Ivan Neville), "Come With Us" (with Clinton Fearon), and "Teach the Youths" (with Sister Nancy).
Under his own production banner, GreenTree Music, and from his recording facility HoveTo Studio on Block Island, Greenlee produced, arranged, and played piano, organ, and clavinet on the 1 March 2022 single "Born a Rastaman" (a reggae arrangement of the Dickey Betts song "Ramblin' Man" featuring vocalist Johnny Dread, guitarist Van Gordon Martin, drummer Nathan Sabanayagam, and percussionist Alejandro Cartin Gulubay, engineered by Will Holland at Chillhouse Studios, mixed by David Goldfine, and mastered by Laurent "Tippie" Alfred). On 1 December 2023, he released the single "Monkey Move" featuring vocalist Toussaint the Liberator, recorded with Will Holland at Chillhouse Studios and Nathan Sabanayagam at Naya Records, and mixed by Alan Evans at Iron Wax Studios. Through his HoveTo Music imprint and HoveTo Studio on Block Island, he subsequently collaborated with vocalist Nena Belén on the singles "There You Are" (2024), "Straight Up" (2025), and "Butterflies" (2026), the last of which was tracked at HoveTo Studio and Rocktorium Records, mixed by Ray Gennari at Rocktorium Records, and mastered by Will Holland at Chillhouse Studios.


ConserFest
In 2008, Greenlee co-founded ConserFest, an annual music and environmental conservation festival held on Block Island that raises awareness and funds for local land and marine preservation concerns, and for which he serves as organizational president and artistic director.

2026 Turkish investment fund scandal, also known as the "2026 Turkish investment fund crisis" is an ongoing political and financial scandal that began in mid-September, when several Turkish investment funds could not meet redemption request from investors. The Capital Markets Board (SPK) subsequently ordered the liquidation of 131 funds run by seven asset managers.  The funds reportedly held approximately $17 billion in assets, affecting about 455,000 investors. Officials have described the structure as a "Ponzi-like" scheme. 
The scandal has expanded into a major political corruption investigation involving figures connected to Turkey's ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP) and senior officials within the country's financial institutions. As of 14 November 2026, the investigation included 464 suspects, 123 of whom had been remanded in custody, including deputy chair of ruling AKP for share trading, head of the Turkish Stock Exchange and former deputy chair of SPK, the latter two on suspicion of bribery. 
The index fell roughly 35% in September, the total market capitalization of publicly traded companies on the stock exchange fell from $479 billion to $306 billion in just two months, causing loss of $173 billion.   


Background
Turkey's central bank kept monetary policy tight in order to reduce inflation and encourages savings in lira-denominated assets, which increased demands for funds promising returns above inflation and deposit rates. Portfolio management industry assets rose form about US$246 billion in August 2025 to about August 2026, according to SPK date cited by Reuters. Some funds became concentrated in a single stock or asset or had very few investors. Some held recently listed small capitalisation shares with low free float, where limited liquidity allows price to move sharply and so lifted the funds' reported daily returns. 
Two asset managers, Tera Portföy and Pusula Portföy, expanded most rapidly. Between August 2025 and August 2026 Tera's assets under management rose more than ten times to US$14.3 billion, and Pusula's thirteen times to US$13.2 billion, making them the sixth and eighth largest portfolio managers in Turkey. Tera's TLY hedge fund reported a cumulative return of over 15,000% in Lira terms by September 2026. It was open in a wider investor base in July and reached more than 100,000 investors and US$5 billion in assets, making it the largest of the funds later liquidated. 


Early warnings
In November 2025 Finance Minister Mehmet Şimşek said that manipulation was being carried out "particularly through certain funds" and that regulatory gaps would be addressed. The SPK chairman at the time Ömer Gönül, warned that managers using funds to circumvent rules could lose their license. On 28 August 2026 the SPK tightened rules on portfolio concentration, related party transactions, valuation methods and hedge fund operation. According to Reuters, the funds continued to grow during this period.


Collapse
In mid-September 2026, funds holding large position in stocks with limited free float struggled to meet redemption requests. Tera Portföy reported difficulties paying investors who wished to withdraw from two funds, and Pusula Portföy later reported similar problems. The SPK suspended trading in the affected managers' funds on the Turkish Electronic Fund Trading Platform (TEFAS) and ordered the liquidation of 131 funds, with a combined volume of about US$18.3 billion. The regulator said that price movement in low-float stocks held by some funds could not be explained. 


Government response
On 29 September 2026 President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan met his economic team and regulators and announced a Fund Coordination Council under Vice President Cevdet Yılmaz to speed up liquidation and payments to investors. The State Audit Institution (DDK) was instructed to examine the funds and the supervisory. The SPK said liquidation would take up to six months. Investors with net holdings below 1 million lira were to be repaid in full, and those with larger holdings were to receive an interim payment of 1 million Lira.
The Savings Deposit Insurance Fund (TMSF) opened accounts for people willing to return what authorities termed as "excessive gains". By 3 November Gürlek said that about 30 billion Lira in money, real estate and shares had been returned through this mechanism. The government also said it was preparing new administrative and criminal rules against market manipulation. Erdoğan declared the crisis under control in mid-October and on 5 November said that some of the people implicated were ones he had not expected. 


Investigation
The investigation is led by the Istanbul Chief Public Prosecutor's Office. On 9 October Gürlek said that 214 people and companies had each made more than 100 million lira in profits from funds managed by Tera, Pusula and Hedef Portföy, totaling about 187.65 billion lira (more than US$3.7 billion). He said that their identities have been passed to the TMSF, which began notifying them to return the money. Investigators focused in particular on investors who sold their holdings between 13 and 16 September 2026, which Gürlek said they had done after receiving advanced information. The number of suspects rose from 217, with 56 in partial detention, in early October, to 433 on 3 November 2026, with 112 under arrest, 186 under judicial supervision and warrants out for 38 fugitives. Assets of 359 individual and 111 entities have been frozen or restricted. 
The same day detention orders were issued for 6 people, including 4 former SPK officials, a serving official of the Banking Regulation and Supervision Agency (BDDK), and a Tera Holding executive. Turkish Stock Exchange (Borsa Istanbul) general manager Korkmaz Enes Ergun, taken into custody on 31 October 2026, was arrested on bribery allegations. Umut Güner, a shareholder in the business daily ''Dünya'', was detained on 1 November and released under house arrest and travel ban. 
On 7 November 2026, four suspects, including former Capital Markets Board (SPK) deputy chairman Enver Usca, were taken into custody. 
On 11 November 2026, three more suspects were arrested as part of the investigation. Those arrested included Yunus Arıncı, former General Manager of Takasbank; Ömer Murat Gülkan, Chairman of OMG Capital Advisors; and Ali Paslı, a founding partner of Adil Participation Bank (Adil Katılım Bankası). Four other suspects detained in the same operation:Enver Çevik, Chairman of Lydia Holding; Nureddin Çevik, Chairman of Ruzy Madencilik; Kemal Akkaya, Chairman of Bulls Yatırım; and Ayhan Yazkan, Chairman of Maygold Otomotiv—were released subject to judicial supervision, including house arrest. The latest arrests brought the total number of people remanded in custody to 123. 


Political fallout


Fatma Betül Sayan Kaya

Kaya, who served as minister of family and social policies from 2016 to 2018, resigned from her AKP posts on September after a opposition politician alleged that she and her husband had profited substantially from trading shares of Ozata Denizcilik before the turmoil. The main opposition party has alleged that she received 2.17 billion lira after investing 163 million lira in one of the funds. The couple had denied the allegations, but according to media reports, transferred some of the profits to an account designated by banking regulators. On 5 November 2026 Kaya was taken into custody waiting trial on charges of membership in a criminal organization and violating the Capital Market Law, according to the state run Anadolu Agency, her husband was released under a reporting requirement. On 9 November 2026, during questioning in connection with the financial investigation, she reportedly declared that she was a homemaker with monthly income of 500,000 Liras (about 10,000 USD).


Opposition
Opposition politicians have called for transparency over who profited and have alleged that current and former ministers are implicated. Erdoğan has said that those responsible would be held accountable, including within his own party.  


Media
On 16 October, the broadcasting regulator RTÜK fined two television channels over coverage questioning the connections of government figures to the scandal.


Economic impact
Besides the fall in the stock market, the changes to the composition of the Borsa Istanbul index removed stocks linked to the crisis. Analysts at JPMorgan said the turmoil posed "meaningful downside risks" to Turkey's 2026 growth forecast of 3%. The total loses to investors remains unclear, as many funds hold thinly traded shares that may be difficult to convert into cash.

Ponzi scheme

From 15 January to 10 February 1910, the January 1910 United Kingdom general election was held in England, to elect all 670 members of the House of Commons, with 461 seats being in England. 456 seats represented territorial constituencies contested under the first past the post electoral system, and several multi-member constituencies under the block voting system; and 5 additional seats represented the 3 university constituencies which returned members under different voting systems.
The election was called amid a constitutional crisis after the Conservative-dominated House of Lords rejected the People's Budget. The Liberal government, sought a dissolution of parliament for the election seeking a mandate, however lost their majority. It was the second of two general elections held during the reign of King Edward VII, due to his death in May 1910.
The election resulted in a hung parliament in which the Conservative Party, along with its long-time ally, the Liberal Unionist Party won the most votes, but with the Liberal Party under H. H. Asquith won the most seats. Another election followed in December as the Liberal government sought a mandate for the Parliament Act 1911, which would prevent the House of Lords from permanently blocking legislation linked to money bills.


Candidates
Below is a table summarising the candidates of the January 1910 general election in England, excluding those in the university constituencies.


Analysis


Liberal Party
The Liberal Party under leader H. H. Asquith saw a severe drop in seats from its high 306 seat count at the previous election. In terms of vote share, the party took 43.0% of the vote, a decrease of 6.0% when compared to the previous election. The party won Hoxton, Darwen and Stockton-on-Tees and one of the two seats in Blackburn from the Conservatives, as well as Great Grimsby, Darlington and South East Durham from the Liberal Unionist Party. It therefore won 7 seats, but with 125 other losses across England to other parties, the party fell to 188 seats in total.   
Within the Liberal Party, several Liberal-Labour MPs that held their seats, with several who had won or kept their seats from the previous election defecting directly to the Labour Party, some of which were successful again their former party while others were defeated by Liberal candidates. Such remaining Liberal-Labour members within the Liberal Party were those in Battersea, St Pancras East, Stoke-upon-Trent, Salford North, Birkenhead, Mid Durham, Morpeth, Wansbeck. As such, the Liberals took 306 seats, within which 8 were held by Liberal-Labour MPs.


Conservative Party
The Conservative Party, led by Arthur Balfour, saw a significant recovery from the previous election, with the party increasing its vote share by 4.2% to 42.0%. The party also recovered in terms of seats, with the party gaining net of 94 seats, with 100 gains and 6 losses. Leader of the Conservative Party Arthur Balfour, who had lost his seat at the last election, re-entered Parliament in City of London at the February 1906 City of London by-election. He retained his seat at this election with an increased vote share. Between the Conservatives and allies, the Liberal Unionist Party, each saw an increase of 111 seats and increase in vote share by 5.0% to 49.3% in England. Although the parties would together get a majority of seats in England, nationally they were not able to form a government against the Liberals. 
The party gained a net of 100 seats alone. However, it lost Hoxton, Darwen and Stockton-on-Tees to the Liberal Party, as well as one of the two seats in Blackburn to the Liberals. It also lost Wigan to Labour, and internally to the Liberal Unionists, it lost St George's, Hannover Square.   
One notable politician to face his first general election was future Prime Minister Stanley Baldwin, who entered Parliament at the 1908 Bewdley by-election following the death of his father and incumbent MP Alfred Baldwin. Baldwin retained the seat for the Conservatives at this election with over 70.0% of the vote.   
Outside of the official party, Independent Conservative Samuel Storey, who stood under the label of Independent Tariff Reform, won one of the two seats in Sunderland, with the Conservatives taking the other. Storey's win was a gain from the Liberal Party.


Liberal Unionist Party
The Liberal Unionist Party, led by Joseph Chamberlain also saw a significant recovery at this election, with a 0.8% increase in vote to 7.3% of the vote. The party retained Totnes, Aylesbury, Ludlow, Burton, Handsworth, East Worcestershire, West Derbyshire and Durham. The party may have retained the seat of North Lonsdale. Seminal collations classed George Haddock as a holding Conservative; however, subsequent research on Haddock's candidature placed him instead as a Liberal Unionist, with the party thereby retaining the seat. 
It also retained its university constituency seats in London University and Oxford University. It also held its metropolitan Birmingham seats of Birmingham Bordesley, Birmingham Central, Birmingham North, Birmingham South and Birmingham West. The party gained East Somerset, Cricklade, Mile End, St Pancras South, Mid Norfolk, West Staffordshire, Ross, Droitwich, Manchester South West. It also gained one of the two seats in Portsmouth.
Of some dispute is the nature of further gains, as to whether they were gains for the Conservative Party or the Liberal Unionists. Seminal collations of the seat counts present 10 of the collective gains for the alliance as a Conservative gains , however, subsequent research into the candidates suggested that each candidate under the already relatively merged Conservative and Liberal Unionist local organisations were, in fact, Liberal Unionists. As such the Liberal Unionists also gained Maldon, Hitchin, Watford, Chertsey, Guildford, Paddington North, Cambridge, Cheltenham, Penryn and Falmouth, and Walsall from the Liberal Party, with each becoming Liberal Unionist MPs rather than winning Conservatives.
Furthermore, in exchange with the allied Conservatives, the Liberal Unionists gained Westminster St George's, Hannover Square from the Conservative Party, with Alfred Lyttelton retaining the seat following the 1906 St George's, Hanover Square by-election after the Conservative incumbent MP Heneage Legge resigned from government and Parliament. 
However, it lost Croydon. Following the death of incumbent Liberal Unionist MP, Hugh Oakeley Arnold-Forster, the Conservative Party won the seat at the 1909 Croydon by-election. It was a gain at this election through the retention of the Conservatives from its ally, although the Liberal Unionist did not stand against the Conservativs. It also lost Great Grimsby, Darlington and South East Durham to the Liberal Party. As such, with 21 gains and 4 losses, the Liberal Unionists increased it seat count to 37 in England, rather than older estimate which placed that party as holding 26 seats. 


Labour Party
The Labour Party which had emerged from the Labour Representation Committee following the last election, increased its number of seats as well as seeing a rise in vote share to 6.9% in England. The party retained its seats of Deptford, West Ham South, Barrow and Furness, Clitheroe, Gorton, Ince, Manchester North East, Newton, St Helens, Westhoughton, Barnard Castle, Bradford West and Leeds East. It also held one of the two seats in Norwich, Leicester, Blackburn, Bolton, Stockport, Newcastle-upon-Tyne and Halifax.
Many of its gains at this election came from the phenomena of several Liberal-sponsored Liberal-Labour incumbents MPs defecting fully to the Labour Party, and retaining their seats, which were gains from the Liberal to Labour. Labour won Hanley from the Liberal Party, with sitting Liberal-Labour MP Enoch Edwards defecting to Labour, and winning his seat from the Liberals. Similarly, in Nuneaton, sitting Liberal-Labour faction MP William Johnson defected to Labour and retained his seat again the Liberals, with James Haslam in Chesterfield, John Wadsworth in Hallamshire and Frederick Hall in Normanton doing the same.
Labour gained North West Staffordshire, Mid Derbyshire, North East Derbyshire, Manchester East and Sheffield Attercliffe directly from the Liberal Party. It gained Wigan from the Conservatives. It also won one of the two seats in Derby from the Liberals. In the seat of Chester-le-Street, John Wilkinson Taylor was elected as an Independent Labour MP, but had joined the Labour Party officially, and stood for it successfully as this election. This represented a further gain for Labour. However, the party lost Chatham, Woolwich, Wolverhampton West, one the two seats in Preston, and one of the two seats in Sunderland to the Conservative Party. It also lost Manchester South West to the Liberal Unionists. With 13 gains and 6 losses, the Labour Party saw it representation in England rise to a total of 33 seats.

Kafushe Kambare, also spelled Kafuxi ka Mbari (Soba Cafuxe in Portuguese) was the title of a historical line of rulers in what is now Angola. The chiefdom of the Kafushe was the most powerful of several in the marshy Quiçama region. They distinguished themselves for having once inflicted a major defeat on the Portuguese during the Angolan Wars, which lingered long in popular local memory. 
The Kafushe Kambare were originally vassals of the king of Ndongo, like the rest of the chiefs of Quissama. Their territory lied in the southern part of Quissama, bordered the Atlantic on one side, and the chiefdoms of Malumba a Kambolo and Longa ria Ndongo on the other. The origins of the Kafushe is unclear, and the Portuguese once camped a detachment of troops on his territory to support him against a rival lord at least as early as 1588. This seems to have been the only time in which a Kafushe Kambare joined the Portuguese in a common cause.

Relations with Luanda must have ruptured shortly afterwards however, as after the salt mines of Quissama were seized by the Portuguese, all the local sobas submitted to them, except the Kafushe. A force of 130 Portuguese soldiers and some cavalrymen supported by an auxiliary force of several allied sobas marched against Kafushe, but although the cavalry succeeded in disorganizing the forces of the Kafushe in a first attack, he fell back to wooded and broken ground. The Portuguese were lured onto uneven ground, surrounded and routed, having suffered severe casualties, including two leading sobas. The Kafushe did not follow up on his victory and the survivors fell back to the Cuanza.
This was the first major setback suffered by the Portuguese in Angola. The Kafushe gained great prestige as a result of his victory and some sobas wished to elect him king in replacement of the ruler of Ndongo, Mbandi Ngola, who thus came to fear him. The rebellion of some Quissama lords along the Cuanza river, followed by the siege of Massangano in 1595 led the Portuguese to establish a new fort at Muxima, on the south bank of the Cuanza, in Quissama territory.
Early in the 17th century, Quissama was invaded by a marauding Imbangala band led by Imbe Kalandula. Kalandula was an ally of soba Langere, a neighbour of the Kafushe. He crossed over the Cuvo river north into Quissama with the aid of the Portuguese. A conflict with Kafushe ensued but it ended inconclusively. Although the Kafushe had to withstand several more invasions of their territory, the Portuguese veteran and chronicler António de Oliveira Cadornega still considered them to be the leading Quissama lord, as although they had once been vassals of Ndongo, they came to hold the prestige of kings themselves, rule over large territories and collected tribute from surrounding lords.
In the early 17th century Governor João Rodrigues Coutinho assembled the "biggest force ever gathered" by Portuguese in Angola up to that point with the aid of the Kingdom of Kongo, in preparation to annex the region of Cambambe from the Kingdom of Ndongo, but he died prematurely. Nevertheless, captain Cerveira Pereira defeated the lords Axila Mbanza and Kafushe Kambare. Governor Manuel Cerveira Pereira negotiated a military access agreement with the Kafushe and thus opened a route to Cambambe, where a new fort was built in 1604. Whether it was after defeating him in battle or by accord, however, is unclear. Nevertheless, the chiefdom of the Kafushe as well as most of the Quissama would remain unsubmitted territory for long afterwards. 
The Portuguese avoided the highly malarial Quiassama swamplands and the Kafushe Kambare chiefdom lingered into the 20th century, but by that point it was in decadence due to epidemics, which reduced the local population from an estimated 40,000 people to only 10,000 by 1930.

The October 2026 Maybrat offensive, codenamed Operation Badai Kasuari 3 (Indonesian: Operasi Badai Kasuari 3) were a series of actions taken by the Indonesian National Armed Forces (TNI) against elements of the separatist West Papua National Liberation Army (TPNPB) in Maybrat Regency, Southwest Papua, on October 2026. The offensive successfully eliminated the bulk of TPNPB forces in Maybrat Regency and decapitated its command structure, including commander Manfret Patem.


Background


Escalation (2021-2026)
Maybrat is one of the six municipalities in Southwest Papua, part of Indonesian Papua. It is the only landlocked regency in the province also its most remote, with its closest point to the ocean (the Seram Sea) being 30 kilometres (18.6 miles) from the coast.
TPNPB units in Maybrat were organised under 4th Regional Defence Commando Sorong Raya (Komando Daerah Pertahanan IV Sorong Raya), which was formed sometime in 2020 in Maybrat, initially under the command of Denny Mos but later taken over by his erstwhile deputy Manfret Fatem. Some of the leading members were originally political activists from the West Papua National Committee (KNPB) chapters in Aifat areas, Maybrat.
Hostilities between the TNI and TPNPB in Maybrat intensified after September 2021 when TPNPB militants under the command of Manfret Patem attacked a TNI post in Kisor, South Aifat district, killing four TNI soldiers. Over the next five years, Manfret Fatem's unit were responsible for numerous attacks on civilian and military infrastructure in Maybrat Regency, including a fatal attack killing one TNI personnel on January 2022, another ambush killing one TNI personnel on December 2023, and an attack on a civilian road construction crew killing four on September 2022. On March 2026, Manfret Fatem's unit killed two TNI soldiers and seized their rifles. Altogether, after the initial TPNPB attack on September 2021, Manfret Fatem's unit were responsible for six TNI personnel and four civilians.
Aside from lives directly lost, the escalation in Maybrat started a major refugee and humanitarian crisis in the area. The National Commission on Human Rights estimated that around 5,200 civilians in Maybrat were forced to flee the area after the initial 2021 attack, and four districts (Far East Aifat, South-East Aifat, Middle-East Aifat, and East Aifat) remained deserted by August 2023. By October 2023, 138 refugees had died, and as of October 2026, 262 refugees remained outside of their homes.


Shifting TNI strategy
After the election of former Minister of Defence Prabowo Subianto as president, the Indonesian government changed its strategy against the TPNPB. On April 2024, the TNI revoked the designation of Armed Violent Group (Kelompok Kekerasan Bersenjata, KKB) from the TPNPB/OPM, a move which according to an analyst justified a shift from a law enforcement-based strategy to a military counter-insurgency-based strategy.
The TNI also shifted its strategy from depending on static military infrastructure (which led to the September 2021 attack) to a proactive tracking strategy and a systematic destruction of TPNPB bases, as well as recapturing weapons which the TPNPB had seized from TNI soldiers killed in ambushes. Under Operation Habema (established 2024), elite TNI units spearheaded destruction of whole TPNPB units, such as in the 2025 attack on a TPNPB base in Soanggama which killed up to 14 TPNPB militants.
On May 2024, 30 militants under Manfret Fatem's unit surrendered to the TNI, severely reducing its strength. By April 2026, it was reported that Manfret Patem unit's core strength had been reduced to seven militants.


Offensive


3 October
On the evening 3 October 2026 in Aifat District, Maybrat, a TNI force came under hostile fire from Fatem's unit after negotiations failed. In the firefight, two TPNPB militants, including Yohanes KY, commander of the 'Karef Hamit Battalion', were killed. Fatem and other militants successfully fled the scene, leaving behind a revolver, blades, and ammunition.


Badai Kasuari 3.10
Over the next few days, TNI forces pursued Fatem's remaining force under the codename Operation Badai Kasuari 3.10. On 9 October, Fatem and his men were spotted hiding in a jungle area in East Aifat District, Maybrat. TNI forces engaged in a firefight with Fatem and his men, and Fatem was killed by hostile TNI fire. His two men, Yesias Fatem (commander of 'Mosata Battalion') and Antonius Assem (commander of 'Ayosami Battalion') attempted to flee by crossing the Kamundan River, but were killed by TNI fire during the river crossing. After the attack, TNI forces retrieved an AK-101 rifle and ammunition. According to TNI sources, the fact that Fatem was discovered by the TNI while only having two men under his command was the result of a significant fracture within his unit which led to him being abandoned by most of his subordinates.


Badai Kasuari 3.12
On 16–17 October, TNI forces under the command of Lieutenant Colonel Laili Nugroho, under the codename Operation Badai Kasuari 3.12, swept jungle areas in Maybrat. During a sweep of the Ayata area on 16 October, the TNI recovered a Caracal rifle, and the next day recovered a Daewoo K3 light machine gun in the Kamundan River area. Both of these were seized by Fatem's unit from the TNI soldiers killed on March 2026. Aside from these weapons, the TNI also successfully seized a homemade gun and ammunition.


Badai Kasuari 3.13
On 29 October 2026, TNI forces the codename Operation Badai Kasuari 3.13 ambushed a TPNPB group at Far East Aifat District, Maybrat. The ambush successfully captured Matius Aisasior, commander of the 'Aikum Battalion' of the TPBPB, his deputy, and another member, without meaningful resistance. It was stated that Aisasior and his battalion were part of Danny Mos' (previous commander of Kodap IV) faction, not Fatem's.


Aftermath
The TNI offensive successfully destroyed virtually all of the TPNPB's organised strength in Maybrat Regency, as well as eliminating the 4th Regional Defence Commando. The surgical strikes conducted by the TNI on TPNPB battalion commanders successfully destroyed the strength of four TPNPB battalions.

Mrigaya 1.5: The Hunt Continues (Bengali pronunciation: [mriɡɔja]) is an upcoming Indian Bengali-language neo-Western buddy cop action thriller film co-written and directed by Abhirup Ghosh. Produced by Tenth Dimension Entertainment, the film is a spin-off sequel to the 2025 film Mrigaya: The Hunt. The film stars Ritwick Chakraborty, reprising his role, alongside an ensemble cast of, Koushani Mukherjee, Mir Afsar Ali, Arpan Ghoshal and Ananya Bhattacharjee, with Vikram Chatterjee in a special appearance.
The story of the film is written by Debashish Dutta, the officer-in-charge of Maniktala Police Station, who was a part in investigating the original incident that was covered in the first film of the universe. It follows four cops who unite to hunt down a "cyber mafia" mafia, that traps young girls and exploit them.
Music of the film is composed by Rana Mazumder. Subhadeep Naskar handled its cinematography, while Sumit edited the film, retained from the first film.
Mrigaya 1.5: The Hunt Continues was released direct-to-video on  Zee 5 on 13 November 2026.


Plot
The film centres on Debanjan Dutta, who investigates a cybercrime case alongside Joyeeta, a capable but short-tempered officer, and Poritosh, a junior officer known for his unusual Bengali vocabulary. Agniswar, Debanjan's mentor and a senior officer nearing retirement, guides the investigation.
A brutal neighbourhood crime draws Debanjan and his team into a dangerous investigation. Under pressure from senior officers, the media and public scrutiny, their apparent breakthroughs expose further danger and deception as a ruthless gang spreads fear and violence across Kolkata.


Cast
Ritwick Chakraborty as OC Debanjan Dutta
Koushani Mukherjee as SI Joyita Sen
Arpan Ghoshal as SI Paritosh Saha
Mir Afsar Ali as ACP Agnishwar Chatterjee
Aritra Dutta Banik as Aritra, a journalist
Ananya Bhattacharya as Sreyanshi Banerjee
Sohini Guha Roy as Maya, Debanjan's wife
Vikram Chatterjee as SI Animesh Roy (Special appearance)


Music
Rana Majumder is associated with the film's music direction. Usha Uthup and Antara Mitra have lent their voices to songs in the film.


Release
The film is scheduled for release on 20 November 2026. BookMyShow and TicketNew identify it as a Bengali-language action-crime thriller, while The Telegraph India described it as a Bangla Zee 5 original film following the release of its trailer on 6 November 2026.


Reception
Mrigaya 1.5: The Hunt Continues received generally positive reviews from critics. Reviewers praised its investigation-led screenplay, the performances of Ritwick Chakraborty, Koushani Mukherjee, Arpan Ghoshal and Ananya Bhattacharya, Soumit Deb's humorous Bengali dialogues and its treatment of technology-enabled crime, while noting that the first half could have been more tightly paced.
Indulge Express gave the film 3.5 out of 5 stars. It praised the film for portraying the evolving nature of digital crime, its suspenseful investigation, Soumit Deb's dialogue, and the performances of Ritwick Chakraborty, Koushani Mukherjee, Arpan Ghoshal and Ananya Bhattacharya. The review singled out Bhattacharya's emotional range as Sreyangshi, calling her one of the film's standout performers, but felt that the make-up could have been better in places and that some cinematic clichés could have been avoided.
The Telegraph India praised the film's central investigation, saying that it effectively expanded from an initial case into a larger web of cybercrime, including self-staged crimes, honey-trapping, extortion, robbery, phishing, image morphing, gang wars and murder. It also appreciated the balance of suspense, action, humour and emotion, along with the restrained performance of Ritwick Chakraborty and the comic timing of Arpan Ghoshal. Sangbad Pratidin described the film as a worthwhile expansion of Abhirup Ghosh's cop universe. It praised the action sequences, particularly the climax, the performances of Arpan Ghoshal, Mir Afsar Ali and Ananya Bhattacharya, and the film's final surprise cameo. The review, however, felt that Aritra Bandyopadhyay's screenplay could have been more gripping in the first half and that some viewers might find the early pacing slow.


Franchise context
Mrigaya 1.5: The Hunt Continues is a spin-off of Mrigaya: The Hunt (2025), rather than a direct continuation of that film's plot. Ghosh has described it as a way to explore Debanjan Dutta's backstory and establish character context before the franchise expands into a larger sequel.

Maia Early Pérez (born October 19, 1999) is an American former professional soccer player who played as a goalkeeper. She played college soccer for the Hartford Hawks before spending three professional seasons in the National Women's Soccer League (NWSL) with Angel City FC and the Seattle Reign, including a loan stint in France with GPSO 92 Issy.


Early life
Pérez grew up in Kentwood, Michigan. She attended East Kentwood High School, where she played four years of both soccer and basketball. She was initially a member of her school's junior varsity soccer team as a freshman before being promoted to varsity later that spring. She finished her career with two all-state honors, including first-team all-state as a junior. As a sophomore in 2015, she led East Kentwood to a Michigan state semifinals appearance. Outside of school, Pérez played for local club Midwest United FC.


College career
After receiving attention from programs at Western Michigan University, the University of Pittsburgh, and Coastal Carolina University, Pérez opted to play college soccer at the University of Hartford. As a freshman with the Hawks in 2017, she started all 19 games and played all but 45 minutes of the season, earning herself a spot on the America East Conference All-Rookie team. The following year, she recorded the most regular-season wins by a Hartford goalkeeper since 2011. Her 0.92 goals against average led the conference, and her 6 shutouts ranked for second. She also played every minute of the season, a feat she would continue throughout her remaining two seasons at Hartford.
In her junior year in 2019, Pérez was named to the All-America East second team and All-New England region third team after leading the conference with a career-best 0.824 save percentage. After the 2020 season was cancelled due to the COVID-19 pandemic, Pérez returned in 2021 for a final year of college. She put on another strong season with a conference-leading goals against average and a shutout record that tied for second. She departed from Hartford ranking eighth in career saves and tenth in career shutouts.


Club career
Pérez registered for the 2022 NWSL Draft, but was not selected. In February 2022, she was invited to NWSL expansion team Angel City FC for a preseason trial stint. The following month, she signed her first professional contract with Angel City, becoming one of DiDi Haračić's backups. After not making any appearances for the team throughout the first half of the season, Pérez was sent out on a one-year loan to French Division 2 Féminine club GPSO 92 Issy. Despite having been signed as Issy's starting goalkeeper, Pérez only made 2 appearances for the club. She returned to Angel City at the end of the NWSL season and announced her intention to retire from professional soccer.
Ultimately, Pérez opted to continue her playing career past 2022. On September 21, 2023, the OL Reign announced that they had signed Pérez as a free agent through the remainder of the season. After being on the Reign roster for the team's final 3 regular season matches and three playoff games, she was re-signed through 2024 at the end of the year. She didn't earn any playing time in her second season with the Reign and was released upon the expiration of her contract.
Pérez has since retired from playing professional soccer.


Honors and awards
Individual

Second-team All-America East: 2018
America East all-rookie team: 2017

George Albert Wingate (24 February 1871 – 2 April 1955) was a military officer, attorney, and judge from Brooklyn, New York. A longtime member of the New York National Guard, he attained the rank of major general and his commands included the 105th Field Artillery Regiment and 52nd Field Artillery Brigade. A veteran of the Mexican Border War and World War I, his awards included the Army Distinguished Service Medal for his First World War service. In his civilian career, Wingate practiced law and was the longtime judge of the Kings County surrogate's court.


Early life and civilian career
George A. Wingate was born in Brooklyn, New York on 24 February 1871, a son of George Wood Wingate and Susan Prudence (Man) Wingate. He attended the public schools of Brooklyn, including P.S. 41. He then studied at the Brooklyn Collegiate and Polytechnic Institute, from which he graduated in 1890. Wingate went on to New York Law School, from which he received his LL.B. degree in 1893. He was admitted to the bar later that year, after which he practiced in Brooklyn.
In 1899, Wingate married Maude Coquelette Lamb of Brooklyn. They were married until her death in 1950 and had no children. Wingate was a Democrat in politics, and in April 1919 Governor Al Smith appointed him to fill a vacancy on the Kings County surrogate's court. He subsequently won election and re-election to the position, and served until retiring in December 1941.


Start of military career
In March 1889, Wingate began his military career by enlisting as a private in the New York National Guard's Company D, 23rd Infantry Regiment. He quickly advanced through Company D's ranks with promotions to corporal, sergeant and first sergeant, then received appointment as the regimental sergeant major. In November 1895, Wingate was appointed a first lieutenant and he was promoted to captain in May 1896. In January 1899, Wingate was assigned as aide-de-camp to Governor Theodore Roosevelt, and he served in this position until 1901.
After his service on Roosevelt's military staff, Wingate was assigned as an assistant adjutant on the staff of the Adjutant General of New York, and he received promotion to lieutenant colonel in March 1903. He served in this position until May 1912, when he was promoted to colonel and assigned to command the 2nd New York Field Artillery Regiment. He commanded the 1st New York Field Artillery from October 1912 until January 2013, when he resumed command of the 2nd New York Field Artillery. In June 1916, Wingate's regiment was federalized during the Mexican Border War, and he commanded first in McAllen, Texas and later at Monte Christo, near the Texas town of Doolittle. He returned to New York with the 2nd New York Field Artillery in December 2016. Wingate was a graduate of the school for field grade officers at Fort Sam Houston, Texas and the US Army Field Artillery School's School of Fire at Fort Sill, Oklahoma.


Continued military career
American entry into World War I occurred in April 1917 and in June, Wingate and the 2nd New York Field Artillery were activated for overseas service and federalized as the 105th Field Artillery Regiment, a unit of the 27th Infantry Division. The division trained at Camp Wadsworth, South Carolina until June 1918, when it departed for combat in Europe. In April 1918, Wingate was promoted to brigadier general and assigned to command the 52nd Field Artillery Brigade. Upon arrival in France, he attended the course at the Camp de Souge Field Artillery Training School near Bordeaux. He went on to lead his brigade during the Battle of Saint-Mihiel and the Meuse–Argonne offensive and he remained in command until the Armistice of November 11, 1918 ended the war. Wingate received the Army Distinguished Service Medal and New York State Conspicuous Service Cross in recognition of his superior wartime service.


Distinguished Service Medal citation
The President of the United States of America, authorized by Act of Congress, July 9, 1918, takes pleasure in presenting the Army Distinguished Service Medal to Brigadier General George Albert Wingate, United States Army, for exceptionally meritorious and distinguished services to the Government of the United States, in a duty of great responsibility during World War I. In Command of the 52d Field Artillery Brigade, 27th Division, General Wingate served with marked distinction in the St. Mihiel operation, displaying military attainments of a high order. In the Meuse-Argonne offensive he proved himself possessed of exceptional tactical ability, working with untiring energy that the Infantry might have all the advantages of Artillery support. With sound judgment, unusual foresight, and wide comprehension of conditions and facilities available, he conducted operations in that offensive with brilliant success, repeatedly solving the difficult problems incident thereto.
Service: United States Army Rank: Brigadier General Regiment: 52d Field Artillery Brigade Division: 27th Division, American Expeditionary Forces Action Date: World War I Orders: War Department, General Orders No. 126 (1919)


Later military career
Wingate returned to the United States in early 1919 and resumed his career with the Organized Reserve Corps. In this role, he frequently took part in training evaluations, inspections, awards presentations, and other activities. On 16 February 1935, with Wingate's military retirement imminent, his decades of military service were commended with promotion to National Guard major general. He held this rank for eight days until reaching the mandatory retirement age of 64 on 24 February 1935.


Later life
In January 1942, Wingate received the Downtown Brooklyn Association Medal to recognize his contributions to the borough's civic life. His professional association memberships included the New York State Bar Association, Brooklyn Bar Association, Rotary Club, and Brooklyn Chamber of Commerce. He also belonged to several legacy societies, including the Sons of the Revolution, Grand Army of the Republic, and Sons of Union Veterans of the Civil War. He was also a member of the American Legion, US Field Artillery Association, Military Order of Foreign Wars, Military Order of the World War, and 27th Division Association. He also belonged to the 105th Field Artillery Officers' Association, 23rd Regiment Officers' Association, 2nd Field Artillery Veterans' Association, New York National Guard Memorial Association, and Forty and Eight.
Additional memberships Wingate maintained included the Society of American Officers, American Flag Association, and West Point Army Athletic Council. Civic organizations and associations in Brooklyn with which Wingate was affiliated included the Boy Scouts of America, American Red Cross, and Elks. He was also affiliated with the National Geographic Society, New England Society, Polytechnic Preparatory Alumni Association, and Public Schools Athletic League. During World War II, Wingate took part in activities to support the US effort, including fundraising drives for the United Service Organizations. He also maintained his interest in Democratic politics; in 1942, he was a member of the veterans committee that supported John J. Bennett Jr.'s campaign for governor. Wingate died in Brooklyn on 2 April 1955. He was buried at Friends Quaker Cemetery in Brooklyn.


Dates of rank
Wingate's dates of rank were:

Private, 18 March 1889
Corporal, 10 February 1890
Sergeant, 11 November 1892
First Sergeant, 22 May 1893
Sergeant Major, 17 September 1894
First Lieutenant, 11 November 1895
Captain, 22 May 1896
Lieutenant Colonel, 28 March 1903
Colonel, 25 June 1912
Brigadier General, 12 April 1918
Major General, 16 February 1935
Major General (Retired), 24 February 1935

Anti-Zionism: A Jewish History is a 2026 non-fiction book written by Benjamin Moser. Published in September 2026, it is a history of Jewish anti-Zionists.


Synopsis
Anti-Zionism: A Jewish History covers the history of Jewish opposition to Zionism through biographical profiles of prominent artists, rabbis, poets, lawyers, activists, journalists, and politicians who challenged Zionism from its birth in the late 19th century to the present. These biographical accounts include Theodor Herzl's contemporary critics, Edwin Montagu and Hannah Arendt. Moser focuses on disagreements over  the moral and political implications of Zionism and the movement's relationship to European nationalism, antisemitism, and the history of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict. Through the varying biographies, Moser argues that Jewish opposition to Zionism has a longstanding and diverse intellectual tradition that challenges the conventional narratives surrounding Zionism.
The Los Angeles Review of Books described the book: "It doesn't break new ground, nor does it intend to, and it is not a 'history' per se. Rather, it is a kind of literary resurrection, a personal search through lost Jewish choices, following paths long overgrown and figures that history thought best forgotten [...] He doesn't set out to be comprehensive, and his argument is not that the lives and ideas of these figures subvert the Zionist vision of history. Rather, they demonstrate how recent a phenomenon the Zionist consensus is.


Publication
The book was published by Doubleday on September 22, 2026. Moser had previously written on the subject of anti-Zionism in The New York Times and The Washington Post.


Reception
Publishers Weekly assessed the book as a "trenchant history of Jewish opposition to Zionism" and said that it "highlights Israel's history of mistreatment of both Palestinians and Jewish minorities like Orthodox Jews and Iraqi Jewish immigrants [...] These illuminating profiles in courage challenge the myth of Zionism's unilateral consensus".
Tom Sperlinger of The Guardian wrote, "It is contentious to oppose Zionism today, but Moser wants contemporary Jewish activists to 'feel less alone' by showing that debates about Zionism were once mainstream in Jewish communal life. [...] Moser's focus on individual lives is affecting. He makes no claims to be writing a definitive history and is aware of the Palestinian lives that shadow those of his protagonists but fall outside the scope of this study. There are nonetheless gaps," going on to cite the Satmar Hasidic movement, which is not mentioned in the book, as an example that anti-Zionism can "also be theological and conservative".
In the Los Angeles Review of Books, Rabbi Shaul Magid wrote, "To give voice to these stories is an act of fidelity to those who suffered at the hands of an emerging national project in a time of trauma."
In his review for The New York Times, Wesleyan University president Michael S. Roth criticized the book as painting "a fraught world in black and white" by overstating Israeli and Palestinian roles as "evil perpetrator and a virtuous victim", respectively, and said Moser "waves away" the historic presence of Jews in the region. Roth describes Moser's writing as "someone whose heart has been broken by his illusions about Jews, and who now wants to make amends by praising Palestinians". He argues that Moser contradicts his own opposition to Zionist claims to the Promised Land by adopting a "sentimental attachment to land-based nationalism" through adherence to settler-colonial theory, which Roth dismisses as "Europeans are bad and non-Europeans are good". Roth further criticizes Moser as not valuing dialogue and believing "with his dualism of good and evil he has achieved 'the correct understanding, which is that there is an occupier and a people under occupation.'" Roth concludes his review by writing that the overall character of Anti-Zionism "is not the mythic anti-rationalism of turn-of-the-century Vienna, but the siren sound of reductionist academic theory" and contends that Moser has merely "transposed the filial piety of his youth to new 'childish things'". In an interview on Piers Morgan Uncensored, Moser responded to the review by calling it "absolutely correct", saying that he does in fact believe the Israeli–Palestinian conflict is black and white and that Zionism as a project was racist from the very beginning.
In The New Yorker, journalist Isaac Chotiner said the book "reminds Jewish Americans that another tradition is available, one that opposes Israel's existence rather than merely seeks to curb its worst impulses". However, Chotiner echoed Roth's conclusion, writing that Moser "merely inverted the triumphalist exceptionalism" of the "fairy tale" history he learned in his childhood. Chotiner continued that "Moser does not detail what a future resolution might entail" and has "no patience for complexity", saying "Moser's biggest failure, though, may be his inability to escape the very solipsism that he denounced in his Equator essay." Chotiner's review also accuses Moser of repeatedly misrepresenting historical sources and making "elementary factual errors" to sustain his thesis.

Anti-Zionism: A Jewish History at Penguinrandomhouse.com

Jackie Davis (born Herman Davis; November 10, 1912 – December 1968) was an American professional boxer who competed from 1929 to 1935. He was inducted into the Greater Cleveland Sports Hall of Fame in 1986.


Early life
Davis was an orphan.


Boxing career
Fighting out of Chicago and Cleveland throughout his career, Davis was well known for wearing the Star of David symbol on his boxing trunks. As an amateur, he competed in the Chicago Golden Gloves in February 1929. He was outpointed in the final by future three-division world champion Barney Ross, whom he reportedly faced five times as an amateur and would go on to face twice more as a professional. Following this, Davis traveled to New York to compete in the Intercity Golden Gloves, defeating James Quinn to capture the featherweight title in the second division, while Ross won the featherweight title in the first division.
Davis turned professional later that year, winning his first eight bouts before facing Ross again on March 17, 1930, losing by newspaper decision over four rounds. On November 11, Davis knocked out Joe Brown in the fourth round. Two fights later, he faced Billy Gladstone in the first of their three career meetings, losing by decision over four rounds.
Davis faced Ross for the final time on March 20, 1931, losing by decision over six rounds. He then went undefeated in his next 18 bouts, culminating in an eight-round decision victory over Gladstone in a rematch on January 8, 1932. His unbeaten streak came to an end when he was outpointed by Harry Dublinsky over eight rounds in Chicago. On September 7, Davis outpointed Gladstone over six rounds in their third and final meeting.
On October 23, 1933, Davis faced former world lightweight champion Sammy Mandell, outpointing him over ten rounds and dealing a setback to Mandell's comeback attempt. Two fights later, he faced future International Boxing Hall of Fame inductee Cocoa Kid in an eight-round bout. Despite being at a disadvantage in height and reach, Davis overcame the handicap with his aggressive, swarming style, keeping Cocoa Kid on the defensive throughout the contest. Davis rallied in the final round to earn a narrow decision victory by a score of 36–35.
On May 18, 1934, Davis faced future world lightweight champion Lou Ambers, suffering a fourth-round knockout defeat. Four fights later, he faced former world featherweight champion Battling Battalino, losing a razor-thin decision over ten rounds. On November 19, Davis outpointed Ray Napolitano in a viciously contested bout that remained evenly matched through the first four rounds, before his superior boxing and sharper punching in the later rounds secured him the decision victory. Davis then suffered a defeat to Joe Rossi, who was subsequently scheduled to face Napolitano but withdrew at the last minute and was replaced by Davis, who outpointed Napolitano for a second time over eight rounds. Two fights later, Davis earned a narrow decision victory over Tony Falco in a grueling ten-round battle.
After outpointing Rossi in a rematch on January 5, 1935, Davis faced Izzy Jannazzo two fights later on January 26, earning an eight-round decision victory in a hard-fought contest in which his aggressiveness proved decisive. The two met again in an immediate rematch, with Jannazzo outpointing Davis over eight rounds. Having been outpointed by Pete Nebo in their two previous meetings, Davis faced him for a third time on April 9, earning a sixth-round technical knockout victory. In his next bout, Davis earned a ten-round decision victory over Frankie Hughes in Miami Beach, Florida. On September 10, Davis outpointed Napolitano in their third and final meeting.
On December 16, 1935, Davis outpointed Press Johnson over eight rounds in what would be his final professional bout. He retired from professional boxing with a record of 74–30–18, including 15 victories by knockout. Between October 1933 and February 1935, he was ranked among the top ten welterweights by The Ring magazine.


Later life and death
Following his retirement from professional boxing in 1935, Davis worked as a boxing referee until 1968, officiating bouts involving world champions such as Sugar Ray Robinson, Jake LaMotta, and Ezzard Charles. He also served as a boxing columnist for the Cleveland Plain-Dealer and Cleveland News and Press. In December 1968, Davis died in the boxing ring while serving as a referee at a charity event.


Personal life
Davis was Jewish.

Guram Amaryan (Russian: Гурам Амарян; born 9 August 1993)  is a Russian stand-up comedian and showman of Yezidi descent, born in Tbilisi, Georgia, who works in Russia. He won the Ukrainian television show Rassmeshi komika, reached the final of the first season of Open Mic on TNT in 2017, and became a regular performer on TNT's Stand Up. He is a host of the YouTube show Reshaly and a producer and host of Po khatam.
His material deals with his Yezidi origin, his family and its traditions, and life in Russia as a young man from the East.


Early life
Amaryan was born in Tbilisi to a Yezidi family. His ancestors fled Turkey for Armenia, and his grandfather later settled in Tbilisi. The Yezidi language was spoken at home, and his parents were religious.
His father was a professional drummer who worked for long periods in Russia. In 2001 the family moved to Novosibirsk, and about a year later they settled in Nizhny Novgorod, where Amaryan grew up. The family of five lived in a 21-square-metre apartment, and his father worked as a seller at the Kanavinsky market, where Amaryan helped him in his final school years.
Amaryan has said that at about fifteen he realised he liked being on stage, and that he was captain of his school's KVN team in the tenth and eleventh grades. After school he expected to work in the tax service. He considered applying to a theatre institute, but entered the finance faculty of Lobachevsky University, where he continued to play KVN and trained as a tax specialist. In a 2024 podcast interview in Armenian he repeated that he had been preparing to become a tax collector.


Career


Early work
To support himself he worked as a shop assistant, a merchandiser, a sales-floor employee at Auchan and, for the longest period, as a courier. Producers of the Ukrainian show Rassmeshi komika (Make the Comedian Laugh) noticed him at a KVN game in Minsk, and he won the programme. He spent the prize money on his first car.
He entered TNT's Comedy Battle as part of a duo, "Sofya-Alyoshka" (Софья-Алёшка), with a friend, Gayane. The duo was eliminated in the second stage. Amaryan has said that after his partner moved abroad he began performing stand-up. His first stand-up performance was at a themed evening in Nizhny Novgorod in 2014. He then became a resident of the Stand Up club in Nizhny Novgorod and worked as an event host.


Television and online shows
In 2017 Amaryan took part in the first season of Open Mic on TNT. He was placed on Ruslan Bely's team and reached the final. He then became a resident performer on Stand Up and has appeared on TNT4's Prozharka.
In July 2021 he was a guest on the YouTube show Chto bylo dalshe? (What Happened Next?). The episode, which had about 22 million views, was removed from the channel, and Amaryan has said he asked the producers to delete it. In 2023 he began appearing with Pavel Dedishchev, Artyom Vinokur and Rasul Chabdarov on Reshaly, a show in which the comedians try to solve problems brought by the audience. He produces and hosts Po khatam with other comedians. In autumn 2023 he became host of the quiz show Narod protiv zvyozd (The People vs. the Stars) on VK Video.
In an episode of Sessiya, a VK Video programme hosted by Dmitry Dibrov, he spoke about being bullied as a child and about his panic attacks. In 2026 the betting company Melbet announced him as a brand ambassador.


Solo shows
His first large solo show, A chto lyudi skazhut? (А что люди скажут?, "What Will People Say?"), was released in 2023. His father, a drummer, opened the performance.
His second show, Gost (Guest), is about a young man who left his homeland more than twenty years earlier in search of success. He toured it in Russia and abroad, including a concert at the Karen Demirchyan Sports and Concerts Complex in Yerevan in February 2024, dates in Uzbekistan in 2024 and in Berlin, Frankfurt and Cologne in February 2025. A closed premiere was held at the Illuzion cinema in Moscow on 19 March 2025, and the show was released on VK Video the next day. In it he jokes that he still feels like a guest in Russia. He has also performed the show in Tbilisi, at the Vakhtang Salaridze Concert Hall.
His third show, Bazar, which deals with marriage, family and everyday conflicts, toured Russian cities in 2026. In a May 2026 television interview he said he gave more than 80 tours a year after his wedding.


Personal life


Yezidi identity

In 2015, ÊzîdîPress described him as the most successful Yezidi in comedy, and Russian media have since called him the first Yezidi stand-up comedian. In the 2015 interview he said it was hard to joke in the Yezidi language and that jokes he translated into it did not sound like jokes, and he expressed a wish to stage the first stand-up concert in his native language. He has said that one of his reasons for going into comedy was to speak about his people. He has also said that for many years he felt like a guest in Russia, that a very small share of Yezidis dislike his work, and that most of the response he hears is positive. He has said that Yezidis have no separate country of their own and that his ancestors were not from Iraqi Kurdistan but from Turkey.
In April 2023 he travelled to Iraqi Kurdistan and visited Lalish, the main Yezidi temple. He called it the holiest place for him and the bastion of Yezidism, and said it was the first time he had been in a place where everyone around him was Yezidi and spoke his native language. He has said that after he arrived in New York, United States customs officers held him for about 40 minutes of questioning. They asked about women's rights in the Middle East and the purpose of his visit, and asked him to tell some jokes from his act. He linked the stop to his trip to Iraq, which he had made on a pilgrimage to the main Yezidi shrine.
In the 2015 interview Amaryan said that he shared the view of Yezidis who oppose marriage outside the community, and that he wanted his wife to be Yezidi. Amaryan became engaged to Eka Mstoyan, then a 21-year-old Yezidi student at the Russian Presidential Academy of National Economy and Public Administration, in May 2025, and the two married in Moscow on 16 August 2025. About 500 guests attended, including comedians and performers, and traditional Yazidi dances were performed. On social media Amaryan said that Yezidi customs were followed: he threw an apple at the bride as a symbol of purity, was given a pillow as a sign of a long marriage, and was given a tree as a symbol of security in marriage. Mstoyan was born in Moscow. The two families had known each other for years. When Amaryan was eight, he attended the wedding of her parents, where his father played music.
