Naihe Bridge (simplified Chinese: 奈何桥; traditional Chinese: 奈何橋; pinyin: Nàihé Qiáo; lit. 'The Bridge of Helplessness'), also called Naihe River Bridge (奈河橋), is a mythical bridge in Chinese folk religion. It crosses the Nai River (奈河), which separates the world of the living from the underworld (Diyu). The souls of the dead are said to cross the bridge on their way to the afterlife. In some stories, good people can cross safely, while evil people fall into the river below.
In some traditions, Meng Po waits near Naihe Bridge and gives the dead a drink that makes them forget their past lives before they are reborn. In the Yuli Baochao (玉歷寶鈔), Naihe Bridge is one of six bridges near the Tenth Court of Hell. Meng Po gives the drink at the Terrace of Forgetfulness (醧忘臺), beyond the bridges.


In literature
The Nai River appears in a Tang dynasty story about Dong Guan (董觀) from Xuanshi Zhi (宣室志). The story is included in Volume 346 of the Taiping Guangji. Dong Guan meets the spirit of a dead monk named Lingxi, who takes him towards the underworld. They reach a narrow river filled with blood. The monk calls it the Nai River and says that it flows from the underworld. No bridge is mentioned.
In Journey to the West, Emperor Taizong of Tang sees three bridges during his visit to the underworld. He crosses a golden bridge, while loyal and good people cross a silver bridge. The third is Naihe Bridge, which stands over a river of blood. It has no railings and is guarded by Ox-Head and Horse-Face. The river is filled with suffering souls, snakes, and iron dogs.
The bridge is also mentioned in the 1925 religious work Dongming Baoji (洞冥寶記). Chapter 19 describes a journey through the Eighth Court of Hell and includes Naihe Bridge in its title.


Real-world bridges
A river called Nai River (渿河) flows through Tai'an, Shandong, near Mount Tai. The nearby Haoli Mountain was traditionally believed to be a place where the souls of the dead gathered. In Shandong Kaogu Lu (山東考古錄), the Qing dynasty scholar Gu Yanwu recorded a bridge across the river near Haoli Mountain. Local people believed that the souls of the dead could not cross it. Worshippers once burned incense and paper offerings there, hoping for protection when they crossed the bridge after death. The old bridges have since been rebuilt.
Another Naihe Bridge stands at Fengdu Ghost City in Chongqing. It consists of three stone arch bridges built during the Yongle period of the Ming dynasty (1403–1424). They were originally part of the Liaoyang Hall complex. The middle bridge is known as Naihe Bridge, while the other two are called the Health Bridge and Wealth Bridge. Beneath them is a pool called the Blood River Pool (血河池). According to local belief, good people can cross the middle bridge safely, while evil people are thrown into the water by ghosts.


Funeral customs
Naihe Bridge also appears in Chinese funeral rites. In Tiediting Suoji (鐵笛亭瑣記), Lin Shu describes a custom in Fujian. On the seventh day of the funeral rites, Daoist priests placed a wooden board over lamps to represent the bridge. They decorated it with banners and made a paper figure of the dead person. The figure was placed in a paper sedan chair, which the family carried across the bridge. The paper objects were then burned.

Georges Maigret (1573–1633) was an Augustinian provincial superior and theologian in the Spanish Netherlands and the Prince-Bishopric of Liège (now Belgium).


Life
Maigret was born in Bouillon in 1573. Aged 17, he joined the Augustinian Order. He was sent to Leuven University to study philosophy and theology, graduating Doctor of Sacred Theology on 17 November 1604. He served as prior of the Augustinian house at Tournai, and later in Liège, where he was the successor to Jean-Baptiste de Glen. He obtained permission from Ferdinand of Bavaria to establish an Augustinian house at Huy, where he also founded a college that existed until 1790. At a chapter held in Enghien on 15 April 1622, he was elected Provincial of the Low-German Province of his order. he would preside at four chapters of the province. In 1624, he became prior in Huy, where he died on 15 May 1633. He was buried in the Church of St George in Huy. He was remembered as a learned man who had great ability and prudence in directing those under obedience to him.

Composite tooling is the set of molds, mandrels and fixtures on which composite material parts are laid up, cured and machined. The tool defines the shape of the finished part and must hold it to tolerance through a cure cycle that, for aerospace carbon-fiber parts, typically reaches about 180 °C (356 °F) under autoclave pressure. The largest, the Invar mandrels on which airliner fuselage sections are built, weigh thousands of pounds.


Requirements
A cure tool must present an accurate surface at cure temperature, so its thermal expansion relative to the part matters: a tool that grows more than the laminate on heating produces a part of the wrong size when both cool, and on large parts the difference can exceed the tolerance. Because composite laminates "spring in" slightly as they cool, tool angles are usually built a degree or two past the nominal part angle to compensate. The tool surface must be vacuum-tight so that no air leaks into the bagged laminate and causes porosity, and it must survive repeated heating, cooling, release-agent application and cleaning without distorting. Lighter tools heat and cool faster, which shortens the cure cycle and makes the tool easier to move.


Materials
The usual tool materials are aluminum, steel, Invar, epoxy- and bismaleimide-based composites, and graphite or carbon foam. Aluminum and steel are cheap and easy to machine but expand far more than carbon-fiber laminates when heated, so they are used mainly for low-temperature cures, prototypes, or where the mismatch can be designed around. Invar, an iron–nickel alloy, expands at close to the rate of carbon-fiber composite up to about 200 °C and withstands thousands of autoclave cycles, which has made it the standard material for high-volume aerospace cure tooling; its drawbacks are cost, weight and lead time, which can approach a year for a large tool. Large Invar tools weigh thousands of pounds; one military tail-cone mandrel is cited at about 15,000 lb.
Composite tools, laid up from carbon-fiber tooling prepreg on a master model, have a thermal expansion close to the part's and weigh roughly 40 per cent as much as an Invar tool of the same size. Epoxy tooling prepreg is limited to a few hundred cure cycles; bismaleimide tooling prepreg survives several hundred to over a thousand cycles at 180 °C and above. Hybrid constructions put a composite or thin Invar facesheet over a carbon-foam or composite backing structure to combine a durable surface with low weight; one such tail-cone mandrel is reported at about 3,000 lb, roughly a third of the Invar equivalent. Machined monolithic graphite is used as a master tool from which bismaleimide working tools are taken; Northrop Grumman has described using this combination for its F-35 composite parts. Nickel shells formed by vapour deposition and machinable tooling boards are also used, the latter mainly for prototypes and low-temperature parts.
Tools have also been made by large-scale additive manufacturing. In 2016 Oak Ridge National Laboratory printed a 17.5-foot trim-and-drill tool for the Boeing 777X wing skin from carbon-fiber-reinforced ABS in 30 hours; Guinness World Records certified it as the largest solid 3D-printed object, and Boeing said the equivalent metal tool from a supplier typically took three months.


Mandrels
Hollow parts such as ducts and one-piece fuselage barrels are laid up on a mandrel that must be removed after cure. For a full-barrel fuselage the mandrel is built in segments that bolt together for layup and retract after cure to free the part. A 4.5 m by 2 m carbon-fiber mandrel of this kind was built for the European FUBACOMP full-barrel business-jet fuselage demonstrator. The Invar mandrels for the Boeing 787's one-piece fuselage barrels are far heavier still.

Sanmao Zhenjun (Chinese: 三茅真君; pinyin: Sānmáo Zhēnjūn; lit. 'The Three Perfected Mao Lords'), also known as the Three Brothers of Mount Mao, are three gods in Taoism. They are Mao Ying (茅盈), Mao Gu (茅固), and Mao Zhong (茅衷). Daoist tradition describes them as brothers who became immortals during the Han dynasty. They are worshipped at Maoshan in Jiangsu, a centre of the Shangqing School of Taoism.


Legends
The brothers were said to have come from Xianyang in present-day Shaanxi. Mao Ying, the eldest, left home at the age of eighteen to study Taoism on Mount Heng. He later met the Queen Mother of the West, who taught him the way to become immortal. His younger brothers, Mao Gu and Mao Zhong, became government officials. They later gave up their posts and went to study under Mao Ying. All three became immortals and settled on Juqu Mountain (句曲山), which was later called Maoshan after them.
The brothers' stories were not always the same. The early text Shangshu Diyanqi (尚書帝驗期) mentions Mao Ying meeting the Queen Mother of the West, but says nothing about his brothers. Their family history and other details were added in later writings.


Worship

The three gods are known by different titles. Mao Ying is the Great Mao Lord (大茅君), Mao Gu is the Middle Mao Lord (中茅君), and Mao Zhong is the Lesser Mao Lord (小茅君). Mao Ying holds the office of Siming (司命), which keeps records of life and death. Mao Gu holds the office of Dinglu (定祿), and Mao Zhong holds the office of Baoming (保命). Their offices also decide who may become an immortal.
The brothers appear in the Zhenling Weiye Tu (真靈位業圖), compiled by Tao Hongjing. The book places them at different ranks among the Daoist immortals. Several Song dynasty emperors later gave them new titles. In 1249, Emperor Lizong of Song granted the brothers further honours at the request of the Daoist priest Situ Shitan (司徒師坦). The orders and ceremonies were collected by Zhang Dachun (張大淳) in the Sanmao Zhenjun Jiafeng Shidian (三茅真君加封事典).
The Jiuxiao Wanfu Palace (九霄萬福宮) stands on the highest peak of Maoshan. It has long been a place of worship for the three gods and received its present name in 1598.
During the nineteenth century, many people from Suzhou, Changzhou, Yangzhou, and Shanghai travelled to Maoshan to worship the brothers. Pilgrims came mainly between the twelfth and third lunar months. Some towns also had smaller temples for people who could not make the journey.
Stone images of the three gods survive at the former Tongxuan Guan (通玄觀) in Hangzhou. They stand beneath an image of Yuanshi Tianzun. The image of Mao Ying is 1.4 metres tall, while those of his brothers are 1.2 metres tall.


Religious texts
The Mao brothers also appear in baojuan (precious scrolls). Some versions tell the story of the Mao brothers, while others name three brothers called Jin Qian (金乾), Jin Kun (金坤), and Jin Fu (金福). In Jingjiang, these stories were recited during local religious gatherings.
An edition of the Sanmao Dijun Baochan (三茅帝君寶懺) was printed in Shanghai in 1924. It contains prayers and texts used in ceremonies for the three gods. It also records a message received through spirit-writing that gave them the title Sanmao Dijun (三茅帝君), or Three Mao Imperial Lords.


References

Totnes Pride is an annual LGBTQ pride event in the town of Totnes in Devon, England. Established in 2013 by Max and Maya Price's organisation Proud2Be, the event includes yearly pride parades, and in 2026 draped a 150-metre (490 ft) pride flag around Totnes Castle.


History
Totnes Pride was originally founded by organisers and identical twins Max and Maya Price, who ran the organisation Proud2Be. The twins moved to Totnes in the early 2010s; Max Price later noted that "There wasn’t anything visible. There was a gay men’s group, so there had always been little groups, but there wasn’t a Pride event, so part of our mission was to increase visibility". As such, they launched Proud2Be in 2011, initially holding monthly social events in the town. The first Totnes Pride event took place on 14 September 2013, and was attended by guests Peter Tatchell, human rights campaigner, and Elly Barnes, an education specialist. It was scheduled to include a panel debate, workshops, talks, and a party at the South Devon Arts Centre.
The second edition of Totnes Pride, on 6 September 2014, included the addition of a temporary rainbow crossing in the town. The crossing was crowdfunded, made of liquid chalk, and placed next to Church Square. The event was larger than in 2013 according to organisers, being attended by hundreds of people. Tatchell returned for a Question Time style debate. In 2015, Proud2Be's proposal for a permanent rainbow crossing was approved by Totnes Town Council and was handed to Devon County Council for approval. 200 to 300 people took part in the 2017 Totnes Pride parade. In November 2018, South Hams District Council blocked the proposal for a permanent rainbow crossing, stating that it would "cause confusion" as people "might not be sure what it was".
The tenth Totnes Pride event, and its first fully in-person event since 2019 due to COVID-19 restrictions, took place on 3 September 2022. It included a march from the Royal Seven Stars which would continue up Fore Street, High Street and Cistern Street to Rotherfold. A Pride market was added for the first time that year.
The 2026 event took the form of a four-day festival with over 40 events. On 26 September as part of this festival, Totnes Castle was covered in a 150-metre (490 ft) rainbow flag, which was unfurled by 30 flag bearers at 10:45 BST to a crowd of 400 people. The installation was titled Capture the Castle, with flag being lent to Totnes by fellow LGBTQ organisation Torbay Pride. Organiser Ceri Goddard stated that the flag was to show that "queer people have always been here and we always will be". Liberal Democrat MP for South Devon Caroline Voaden expressed support for both the installation's message and the fact that Totnes Pride would "boost our local economy and growing profile as a centre for progressive arts and culture." The installation saw criticism on social media from some Totnes residents over the idea that the flag would diminish the historical significance of the castle. Restore Britain leader Rupert Lowe described the flag on Facebook as "wildly naff", "generally vomit-inducing", and "woke theatre". Totnes Pride organisers responded that the negative response to the flag "has been exclusively online and nearly all emanating from outside Totnes", while event attendees saw the event as "wonderful".
